Artificial Broadcasts as Galactic Populations: II. Comparing Individualist and Collective Bounds on Broadcast Populations in Single Galaxies
Brian C. Lacki

Abstract
The search for extraterrestrial intelligence includes efforts to constrain populations of artificial broadcasts in other galaxies. Previous efforts use individualist methods, searching for single broadcasts with high signal-to-noise ratio. These would be detected as observables with extreme values. This approach is limited to very bright broadcasts and also is subject to confusion, where a large number of broadcasts blend together to form a noise continuum. The mean value of the total emission provides an additional collective bound: the luminosity of the transmitters is no higher than the galaxy’s observed luminosity. Using the framework developed in Paper I, I evaluate how confusion affects individualist searches. I then compare individualist and collective approaches for radio broadcasts from the Milky Way, M31, and three Virgo Cluster elliptical galaxies. For current observations, confusion blurs narrowband radio broadcasts together in the Virgo ellipticals when there is one broadcast per gigahertz per 1000 stars. The collective bound implies fewer than ∼ 10 6 ​ ( ℓ ¯ / 10 13 ​ W ) − 1 \sim 10^{6}(\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}/10^{13}\ \textrm{W})^{-1} L-band broadcasts per star gigahertz GHz in the Milky Way and is about 10 10 and 400 400 times stronger in M31 and M59, respectively. Applying the collective bound to the far-infrared–radio correlation yields constraints on radio broadcast populations in star-forming galaxies throughout the Universe. The collective bound allows us to rule out large regions of broadcast population parameter space even for distant galaxies. It also imposes constraints on gamma-ray, neutrino, and gravitational-wave broadcasts in the nearest galaxies.



I
Introduction

A central debate in the search for extraterrestrial intelligence (SETI;
Tarter 134
,
Worden et al. 144
) is whether interstellar travel boosts the number of broadcasting societies. Although realistic travel times between the stars are long, they are miniscule compared to the age of the Galaxy. If ETI societies can reliably replicate through interstellar travel and any have the motivation, then the Galaxy could be covered within about a hundred million years (e.g.,
Jones 71
,
Wright et al. 147
;
Carroll-Nellenback et al. 20
). Essentially, the Galaxy would experience a “phase transition” between unpopulated wilderness and a “metasociety” of densely packed ETIs (Paper I, Lacki 2024 in press; compare
Kuiper & Morris 78
and
Ćirković & Vukotić 27
). The apparent lack of evidence for so widespread ETIs in the Milky Way and its implications is the subject of much debate (
Brin 15
;
Webb 141
;
Ćirković 26
;
Forgan 47
,
Lingam & Loeb 92
).

Less attention has focused on the technosignature properties of other galaxies if this reasoning is correct, aside from searches for megastructure populations
[
9
,
139
,
147
,
150
]
. If ETIs are rare but establish a vast number of societies when they occur, then some galaxies may be heavily populated while others are uninhabited. This should carry over to their technosignatures – some galaxies would be entirely barren of them, while others would be brimming with the signs of billions of inhabited worlds. A negative SETI result for one galaxy might mean nothing for another (Paper I).

Figure 1:
A sketch of how individualist and collective bounds probe the broadcast luminosity distribution in different ways. On left is a standard luminosity vs. abundance plot for broadcasts, as seen in many SETI papers. There is a single individualist bound (purple), a result from a typical SETI search, which rules out model metasocieties B and C. A collective bound (grey) rules out model metasocieties A and B. The middle (individualist) and right (collective) panels show the relevant cumulative distributions. Any distribution that touches the shaded regions is ruled out. The individualist bound is a very sensitive probe of the cumulative number of broadcasts
brighter
than a luminosity sensitivity, but it has no sensitivity below that, even if there are very many broadcasts as in model A. Not shown are the effects of confusion, which happens when broadcasts are so numerous as to blend together. The collective bound is weakly sensitive to populations of all luminosities but is unable to constrain model C, where there is a moderate number of very bright broadcasts. Note that the collective bound’s “power” is the same for models A and B, despite A having far more broadcasts, because those broadcasts are much dimmer.

This paper considers the constraints we can set on broadcasts from an individual galactic metasociety. Thus far, radio and optical SETI surveys treat broadcasts as potential rare anomalies that must be sifted out from natural sources, noise, and local interference, employing strategies that look for
individual
candidates that stick out from this background (e.g.,
Oliver & Billingham 104
,
Howard et al. 68
;
Enriquez et al. 44
). The individualist approach exploits extreme values in the broadcast population statistics. This paper introduces a complementary
collective
approach of using the integrated luminosity of the population: all the broadcasts in the galaxy put together cannot outshine the galaxy’s observed total emission (Figure
1
).

Constraints based on aggregate emission are a general strategy for noticing and setting limits on new or unknown phenomena
[
53
,
41
, e.g.,]
, particularly in astroparticle physics
[
123
,
51
, as in]
. In SETI, they let us trade abundance with brightness – a given aggregate luminosity can be achieved by a few very bright broadcasts or a great many faint ones – and allow us to constrain faint transmissions even in distant galaxies. The collective emission of an ETI population can also be relatively insensitive to the detailed properties of individual transmissions thanks to the central limit theorem. We can use the collective bound to set limits on signals that do not remotely resemble the classical pulses or carrier waves (
Kardashev 73
,
Caves & Drummond 22
;
Lacki 80
), even ones that look like white noise, obviating the need for a “magic frequency” or “magic basis.” Finally, they are not subject to confusion: when an observation covers many broadcasts, they can all blend together into a noisy background with none individually detectable. It is this quasi-continuum that collective bounds best constrain.

Indeed, the collective approach follows in the footsteps of searches for
Kardashev [73]
Type III societies in other galaxies. This branch of SETI seeks the collective effects of millions of inhabited solar systems on the galaxy as a whole, like the waste heat or stellar obscuration from populations of megastructures. Obviously no such ubiquitous population exists in the Milky Way (
Jugaku & Nishimura 72
,
Carrigan 19
;
Suazo et al. 127
), but this does not preclude Type III societies in other galaxies
[
147
]
.

I present a comparison of the relative strengths and weaknesses of the individualist and collective bounds for radio broadcasts in individual galaxies, including a discussion on confusion. Although the focus here is on galaxy-wide populations, collective bounds could be applied to individual stars or planets, even in the absence of interstellar travel. For example, Earth has a multitude of radio transmitters
[
129
]
, which could become so numerous and wideband as to be hopelessly confused – yet Earth could still appear anomalous if it is far too radio-bright for a terrestrial planet.

I.1
Outline of Paper II

The formalism of Paper I is reviewed in Section
II
, and then applied to the measured aggregate emission of metasocieties in Section
III
. The next three sections deal with the individualist, signal-to-noise-based constraints on ETI broadcasts and how they are weakened by confusion: general considerations in Section
IV
, then the signal-to-noise ratio for radio broadcasts in Section
V
and optical broadcasts in Section
VI
. The collective bound is explained in Section
VII
. I consider the relative merits of individualist surveys and the collective bounds for some nearby galaxies in Section
VIII
. Section
IX
expands the discussion of the collective bound to distant galaxies across the spectrum. The conclusions, in Section
X
, are followed by several appendices with detailed derivations.

II
Review of concepts

Paper I developed a treatment of ETI populations, but this section reviews basic results used in this paper. At the heart of the formalism is the idea of describing broadcasts and their host societies with random variables. Random variables are a very flexible concept – even a deterministic quantity can be viewed as a random variable with a degenerate distribution. It is likely that at least some properties of technosignatures are unpredictable to us. The time and circumstances in which an ETI evolves on a planet, the exact times it chooses to broadcast and for how long, and the random locations of stars in a galaxy are all contingent, and are among the motivations for a statistical treatment.

Table
1
is a key to the most commonly used variables and notation in the paper.

II.1
Objects: From the universe to broadcasts

The framework interprets populations as nodes on a tree, each level representing a different type of object. Parent objects may host child objects, which form a population of the child level’s type. In variables, each type of object is denoted by an uppercase level (
\mathrm{J}
,
\mathrm{K}
, and
\mathrm{L}
for arbitrary types to describe general relations). The object trees are random but can be characterized statistically. The tree is rooted in the model universe (type
\mathrm{U}
), which contains all other objects, and then has four levels of objects below it. The universe contains galaxies (object type
\mathrm{G}
), which in this work are assumed to totally confine ETIs, even with interstellar travel.
1
1

1

I
If there is enough communication or travel between galaxies to allow for intergalactic metasocieties, that could allow for homogenization, in which case the constraints that apply for one galaxy could apply to its neighbors. However, it could also mean that all galaxies near the Milky Way are atypical and that their technosignature properties do not reflect those of the Universe at large.

ETIs have the capacity to reproduce themselves, their infrastructure and environments, and their technosignatures. A
metasociety
(object type
\mathrm{M}
) is a collection of ETIs generally sharing a common origin or influence, and can comprise one or many worlds. All of the societies originating from a single origin through replication share a metasociety. Depending on the scenario, we may treat the galaxy as having one unified metasociety or many small ones (see Paper I). Metasocieties embody the “phase transition” of a galaxy going from uninhabited to fully populated.

A
society
(object type
\mathrm{C}
) is a localized ETI with its infrastructure, capable of producing technosignatures. A natural interpretation is that of a single world or planetary system, although isolated facilities in interstellar space making broadcasts can also count as societies.

A
broadcast
(object type
\mathrm{B}
) is an emission of radiation produced as the technosignature of a society. These include deliberate attempts at communication with other (meta)societies, leakage, and even noncommunicative releases of energy. Broadcasts are characterized by their time and frequency ranges, among other properties.

The objects, as nodes on the tree, can be treated in two ways. Sometimes we want to consider objects that are known or postulated to exist, like the Milky Way or the Arecibo message. These are realized objects, and their properties have fixed values. They are labeled either using an obvious designation for the object in question (e.g.,
\mathrm{MW}
for the Milky Way), or by the lowercase letter for the object’s type (e.g.,
\mathrm{m}
for a generic realized metasociety). Often, however, we will consider objects whose properties are general random variables. These are random objects, labeled by the uppercase letter for the object’s type (e.g.,
\mathrm{M}
for a random metasociety). Variables can describe both realized and random objects, and both types can be considered hosts of descendant objects, so most relations that work for one kind work for the other (see Paper I for technicalities).

The tree structure accounts for the influences between the objects, with influences running from parent to child instead of between “siblings.” A key assumption of this work is that the properties of all the children objects of a parent are independent of each other, conditionalized on the properties of the host. For example, the active times of broadcasts from a single society are not independent, because if we know when one happens, we have an idea when the society was active and a guess for when it made other broadcasts. But if we already know when the society was active, knowing the time of one broadcast provides no new information. The basic motivation for this is that it lets us treat the effects of shared history or influence between objects while using assumptions of (conditional) independence to simplify analysis. Thus, all the broadcasts in a society may be at a single designated frequency; this is viewed as a property of the society that all the broadcasts independently draw on, rather than a dependent shared property of the broadcasts. This conditional independence allows us to invoke certain results like the central limit theorem, which applies to the sum of all the emission from individual broadcasts, and then applies again to the sum of the emission from all the societies.

II.2
Haystacks and point processes

The intrinsic properties of each object
\mathrm{J}
are given by a tuple of parameters
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Programs like SETI that seek out rare objects in a vast abstract space of possibilities often refer to seeking these out as “needles in a cosmic haystack” (
Wright et al. 146
; see also
Harwit 61
,
Djorgovski et al. 40
. In allusion to this, the parameter space of all tuples for each object type
\mathrm{J}
is its
haystack
, denoted
\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}
. For example, the broadcast haystack’s dimensions include the bandwidth, duration, starting time, energy release, and polarization properties, among others.

Any population of objects is modeled as a point process on the appropriate haystack. A point process is basically a random set of points in a space
[
75
,
37
,
10
,
24
,
57
, for details, see]
. The entire population of
\mathrm{J}
-objects hosted by a single ancestor
\mathrm{K}
on the tree is the point process denoted
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
(a realized object can be substituted for
\mathrm{K}
).
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
is a random variable counting the number of objects in
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
.

A distribution (or “intensity”) of objects on the haystack gives the mean number of objects. For the
\mathrm{J}
-type objects sharing the ancestor
\mathrm{K}
, the distribution is denoted
\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
. The mean number of objects in a subset
\mathnormal{A}\subset\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}
is,

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\mathnormal{A})\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{A}}\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(1)

In general, the distribution depends on the properties of the ancestor
\mathrm{K}
, which are specified by the tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
. This lets all the child objects share statistical properties. If the ancestor is a realized object
\mathrm{k}
, then
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
is fixed to
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{k}}
, and the only remaining dependence of the distribution is on
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
itself.

A Poisson point process is one in which the number of points in any region has a Poisson distribution and non-overlapping regions are independent
[
75
]
. It is appropriate when objects appear independently of each other according to a single well-defined intensity. In this paper, the broadcasts of a realized society and the societies of a realized metasociety are modeled as Poisson point processes: the properties of the immediate “parent” of each subpopulation fully specify the intensity. But when considering the descendant objects of a higher-level ancestor, the distribution of objects itself is random, because it is a random sum of distributions from each of the parents hosted by that ancestor. A Cox point process is just this sort of random superposition of Poisson point processes
[
75
]
. In this paper, the broadcasts of a metasociety (or galaxy) are modeled generally as a Cox point process.

II.3
Selections

The two basic steps from the statistical description of a population to an observed quantity are selection and measurement. Selection draws a sample. Measurement generates an observable quantity from the gross properties of the sample, introducing noise variance from background noise and microscopic fluctuations in radiation.

Selections are central to the framework (Paper I). They sieve through the vast panoply of objects in all the universe’s history, reflecting the limited scope of our programs. A selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K})
consists of a window
\mathsf{x}
and a host object
\mathrm{K}
.

Windows filter objects solely according to their tuples’ positions in the haystack. They include the probability that an object at a given location will be selected, essentially the completeness of the selection. A common type of window selects things according to fundamental quantities like time (
\mathsf{t}(t)
) and frequency (
\upnu(\nu)
). Other examples of windows include observations (
\mathsf{o}
) and surveys (
\mathsf{s}
). Windows also can filter emission and other quantities, defining bounds of integration: for example, the energy fluence of a broadcast intercepted during one observation can be much smaller than the energy fluence over the entire survey, or all of history. If no window is specified for a random variable, the special ALL-window
\infty
is used by default, picking every object and all emission regardless of parameters.

The host specifies a subpopulation of objects to be selected: objects that are not descendants of the host on the “tree” are not included. The host object is specified by its index; when none is given for a random variable, the universal host
\mathrm{u}
(or
\mathrm{U}
) is assumed by default. In order for an object to be selected by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K})
, its tuple must be a member of
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
.

The random set of
\mathrm{J}
-type objects passed by a selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K})
is the random sample
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
. A
\mathrm{J}
-type object passes this selection if its tuple would be included by the window
\mathsf{x}
, and the object also happens to be a descendant of
\mathrm{K}
. The selection window modifies the distribution of objects within the host to
\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
, which gives the mean number of sampled objects:

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(2)

II.4
Random variables and selection-dependent properties

Singleton variables are random variables describing single objects, like the energy released by an individual broadcast during an observation. The notation for singleton variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}
for object
\mathrm{J}
has the form
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
, with the variable itself a lowercase character. Generally, the singleton variable is integrated within the bounds of some window
\mathsf{x}
; a singleton variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}
missing its quantity window integrates without external restriction.

Aggregate variables are the sum of singleton variables for a subpopulation of objects drawn by a selection:

{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}.}

(3)

Number variables
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
are special cases of aggregate variables for which
{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}}=1
. The distinction between an aggregate variable and a singleton variable is, in the end, one of interpretation. The sum of a quantity among all the objects hosted in a parent can be viewed as a variable associated with the parent itself:
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{K}}\leftrightarrow\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
. This interconversion lets us transfer definitions between singleton and aggregate variables.

The distribution of
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
is fully determined by its parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
, but the value it actually takes can be otherwise random. So while the effective isotropic luminosity of a broadcast,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
, is an innate property, the number of photons we count from inevitably has shot noise that we cannot predict. The simple mean of a random variable is defined:

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle{.}

(4)

The simple variance follows from the definition of variance:
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{2}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle^{2}
. The definitions apply analogously for aggregate variables. Campbell’s formula lets us find the simple mean of an aggregate variable, applying to any point process:

\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}

(5)

[
75
,
10
, e.g.,]
.

But we may instead want to know the statistics over all possible objects drawn from a sample – say, the mean number of photons we collect from a random broadcast sampled by a survey. Given a selection
(\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K})
, the selection-relative probability density function (PDF) of a variable is

\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle}\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](x)d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathchar 59\relax

(6)

where
\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](x)
is the unbiased PDF for
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
at
x
, implicitly conditionalized on
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. The statistical properties of objects and populations can change depending on how they are selected: selections introduce bias. The selection-relative mean is derived from the selection-relative PDF and is defined as

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle

(7)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{K}}\right\rangle.

If the values of
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
among different objects are independent of each other and the number of objects, then

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}=\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle}\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(8)

Selection-relative variances are defined using the selection-relative means:
\mathbb{V}\left[X\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}=\left\langle X^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}-\left\langle X\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{2}
.

In this work, almost always the selection window is the same as the window in the variable. If we want to know the “average number of photons collected from a broadcast in an observation”, usually what we mean is the average number from a broadcast sampled by that observation – we do not care about all the broadcasts that happened a billion years ago that we measure no photons from. Hence, the selection-relative PDF, means, and variances “inherit” the variables they are averaging over:
\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{L}}=\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}
,
\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}
,
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{L}}=\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}
, and similarly for aggregate variables.

When
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
is Poissonian, and the individual
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
for each object in the population are identically distributed, mutually independent, and independent of
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
,
\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
has a compound Poisson distribution. This means it has a mean and variance of

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}

(9)

[
7
,
11
,
12
]
. We can use this to calculate the mean and variance of the aggregate emission of all broadcasts in a society and the aggregate emission of all societies in a galactic metasociety (see Paper I).

II.5
Working with surveys: regularization and multiwindow operations

A survey
\mathsf{s}
typically consists of many observations
\mathsf{o}_{i}
, perhaps grouped into pointings at different locations in the sky. The set of observations that make up the survey is
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}
. A lot of the questions we are interested in depend on the variations between observations in the survey: “What is the brightest broadcast fluence in an observation over the entire survey?”, for example, or “What is the variance of an observable we measure in a survey?”

The first tool invokes the finite reach of surveys. This becomes important when we consider probability distributions with very long tails. Broad fluence distributions naturally arise if there is a wide range in the intrinsic emission of individual broadcasts, their distances, or, for narrowband lines, drift rates, for example. Certain power laws can have infinite variances and means because of these heavy tails. In practice, however, we are interested in the means and variances of a typical sample. To do this, we can regularize random variables by truncating their distributions at values unlikely to be sampled:

\uppsi[{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{[\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}]}}](\mathnormal{\kappa})\equiv\frac{\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](\mathnormal{\kappa})}{\mathnormal{P}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H})}\mathbb{I}[\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H}].

(10)

The values of
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H}
and
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}
are derived from the probability distributions of the maximum and minimum
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
in the sample
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
using extreme value theory. The probability that the maximum
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
, of all the
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
values sampled, is above
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H}
is a constant (
1/4
in Paper I); the probability that the minimum
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
is below
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}
is also a constant (again,
1/4
in Paper I). Thus,
{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}
is the fluence of a broadcast intercepted by the observation
\mathsf{o}
, regularized to only include broadcast fluence values likely to be measured in the survey as it covers the metasociety
\mathrm{M}
.

The other issue is that sometimes we need to find a mean, variance, or maximum of some quantity over a group of observations. The multiwindow mean and related observations generalize selection-relative operations. Selection-relative operations are defined for a single window, while multiwindow operations consider the range of values over many windows. The multiwindow mean is defined

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}\equiv\left\langle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle.

(11)

The multiwindow mean can be combined with the selection-relative mean for a host,

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{L}}\equiv\left\langle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}}|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle.

(12)

The multiwindow variance follows from the definition of conditional variance:

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\langle{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle+\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}\right]

(13)

This expression of the law of total variance says that the multiwindow variance results from both the average variation of the variable within each subwindow and the variation between them. Finally, we have multiwindow maxima, which are useful in defining maximum signal-to-noise ratio:

\displaystyle\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{L}}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{max}[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}]|\mathsf{o}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}]

(14)

Of course, an aggregate variable can be used in place of the singleton variable in this equation as well.

II.6
The box and chord models

The box and chord models give us analytical results for the emission properties of broadcasts. In both models, the window
\mathsf{x}
is treated as a contiguous “box” covering the time range
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\leq t\leq\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
, the frequency range
\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2\leq\nu\leq\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2
, and
|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|
independent polarizations (
p
) from the set
\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Here
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
is the duration of the window,
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
is its bandwidth,
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}
is the starting time of the window, and
\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}
is its central frequency.

Broadcasts may fall into one of four categories according to their duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
and (instantaneous) bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
, relative to that of the window. Lines are broadcasts that are narrowband and long-lived (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
;
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
), while pulses are wideband and short-lived (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
;
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
). Less commonly considered in SETI are the hisses, wideband and long-lived continuum sources (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
;
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
), and the blips, narrowband transients (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
;
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
). The box model is used for pulses, hisses, and blips, while the chord model allows for the treatment of lines with frequency drift.

II.6.1
The box model

The box model treats broadcasts as uniform “boxes” in time-frequency space as well, all with a single duration
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
and bandwidth
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
. Each broadcast begins at time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}
and is centered at frequency
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
. There is no skewness to the box, no drift or dispersion. The (effective isotropic) spectral luminosity per polarization of broadcast
\mathrm{B}
is unvarying within the box:

\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)=\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p)\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\\
{\cdot\mathbb{I}[0\leq t-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}]\cdot\mathbb{I}[|\nu-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}/2)]}\mathchar 59\relax

(15)

where
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
is the effective isotropic energy released during the broadcast,
{\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}}(p)
of which is into polarization
p
. A broadcast is selected by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J})
if its “box” overlaps the window’s “box” and it is part of
\mathrm{J}
’s broadcast population. The (effective isotropic) energy integrated over the overlap,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
, is proportional to the time-frequency “area” of the overlapping region:

{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}=\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\sum_{p\in{\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p){=\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(16)

where
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}
is the length of time and
{\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}
is the span of frequencies in which both the broadcast and the window are active. In the box model, the expected number of intercepted broadcasts in host
\mathrm{J}
(regardless of detectability) is

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\approx\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}){\cdot}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})\mathchar 59\relax

(17)

for a broadcast frequency rate per star of

\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)=\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}(t)\right\rangle}\frac{d^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}=t\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}=\nu)\mathchar 59\relax

(18)

where
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}(t)
is the number of stars existing in the host
\mathrm{J}
at time
t
and
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}
is the number in
\mathrm{J}
covered by the window
\mathsf{x}
.

II.6.2
The chord model

The chord model treats broadcasts as ultranarrowband lines that drift linearly in frequency. Thus, each broadcast acts like a “chord” cutting across the window “box.” Each broadcast’s drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
is constant, but the population of broadcasts has a whole distribution of drift rates. In this work, I adopt the uniform drift rate distribution,

\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}=1/(2\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}})\cdot{\mathbb{I}[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}]}.

(19)

Note this is the distribution unbiased by any window selection; narrowband windows are more likely to pick broadcasts with high drift rates. The chords have no intrinsic luminosity variability (but see Section
V.4
). Therefore, the total amount of emission from broadcast
\mathrm{B}
intercepted during the window is directly proportional to the time it takes to cross the box,
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. Each broadcast has an effective isotropic luminosity
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
; it releases

\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle=\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\sum_{p\in{\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p){=\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}

(20)

as energy during the times, frequencies, and polarizations covered by the observation
{\mathsf{x}}
. Selection by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{J}})
happens when a broadcast has
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}>0
and is part of
{\mathrm{J}}
’s broadcast population.
2
2

2

As in Paper I, the broadcast’s parent society, metasociety, and galaxy must also be selected by the window, although that can be assumed for any broadcast falling within the window for this paper.
From this criterion, the expected number of intercepted broadcasts is

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}){\cdot}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})\mathchar 59\relax

(21)

using the instantaneous broadcast frequency abundance per star

\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)=\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}(t)\right\rangle}\frac{d^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t)\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}=\nu)\approx\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}\mathchar 59\relax

(22)

with
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t)
equal to the number of active broadcasts in
\mathrm{J}
at time
t
.

II.7
Assumptions Used in This Paper

A number of simplifying assumptions are used in this paper to make calculations tractable, summarized in Table
2
.

Table 2:
Standard Assumptions Used in This Paper

Assumption

Description

Independence

Objects sharing the same “parent” have independent properties, conditionalized on the parent’s properties. The number of objects is independent of their properties.

Interchangeability

The broadcast distribution in one society at one epoch is identical to that in another society or epoch, except translated in space and time.

Interchangeable observations

Within a single metasociety, the statistical properties of broadcasts and societies are the same in every observation considered, regardless of frequency, time, or location.

Single metasociety

Each galaxy has one metasociety; one distribution characterizes all broadcasts and societies in it. Background and foreground galaxies are ignored.

Diffuse approximation

The number of sampled broadcasts per society is typically
\ll 1
. Their clumping into societies is ignored, as if the broadcasts themselves are scattered in a diffuse cloud throughout the galaxy according to a Poisson point process.

Distant galaxy

All objects within a galaxy are at the same distance and the same dilution (fluence-to-emission ratio).

Negligible extinction

No emission is absorbed or scattered en route to Earth (
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=1
).

Uniform beam

The instrumental response is uniform across the bandwidth and duration of an observation and across its footprint on the sky (
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}={\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
).

In this paper, I consider constraints on broadcasts in individual galaxies, specifically their abundance and brightness. All intercepted broadcasts are assumed to arise from the one galaxy being targeted. This is important because observable quantities like energy received or photons counted cannot tell if the broadcasts are from within the galaxy or not.

There are several different ways of interpreting metasocieties described in Paper I, each reflecting different assumptions about the mutual influence and interstellar replication of societies. In this paper, I adopt the simplest scenario, the single metasociety assumption (
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{M}}=1
). We cannot observationally distinguish a galaxy with a metasociety with sufficiently few technosignatures from one with no metasociety. Thus, for this paper, uninhabited galaxies have trivial metasocieties with no societies and no broadcasts. The practical effect is to ignore a term in the variance of observables related to the “clumping” of broadcasts into discrete metasocieties. The metasociety can be more or less identified with its host galaxy, but I consider astrophysical properties like number of stars to be fixed parameters of the host galaxy. The metasociety
\mathrm{M}
, on the other hand, contains the parameters describing the ETIs and their broadcasts; these are unknown variables, and the metasociety is considered to be random. SETI programs aimed at galaxies can be viewed as trying to constrain these unknown metasocietal parameters.

Societies in the metasociety are described by their position
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}
, and origin time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}
. Furthermore, it is assumed that societies trace the stellar population, at least in bulk (see discussion in Paper I), with an abundance per star of
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}
. When the metasocietal properties are fixed, societies are described by a Poisson point process with distribution

\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}=\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\frac{d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}})\right\rangle}}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}})\\
\cdot\uppsi[\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}}].

(23)

The
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}
collects societal-level properties of the broadcast distribution, like a shared luminosity.

Each society can host broadcasts, which are characterized by their energy release
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
, quantities relating to polarization of the broadcasts (
\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}
), a position (
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}
) identical to that of the transmitting society, and various parameters describing their time/frequency behavior. The distribution of broadcasts for each society is of the form

\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}=\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})})\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}\\
\cdot\begin{cases}\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}})&(\text{box})\\
\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}\updelta(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}})\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}&(\text{chord})\end{cases}.

(24)

Each realized society’s broadcasts also are described by a Poisson point process. I further adopt an interchangeability assumption: the statistical properties of the broadcasts of one society are equivalent to those of another, aside from translation in time and space. Hence, every society has the same broadcast energy release distribution
\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}
and the same polarization distribution
\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}
, and all share the same frequency rate or abundance (
\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}=\left\langle\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
in the box model;
\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}=\left\langle\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
in the chord model). When I need to pick a
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
distribution in this work, I assume that all broadcasts have the same effective isotropic energy, and, when relevant, luminosity (also known as effective isotropic radiated power or EIRP):

{\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}=\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}=\updelta(\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}}).}

(25)

To simplify matters further, I often employ the diffuse approximation, which ignores the discreteness of societies. This is valid if there are many societies, dividing up a few broadcasts among them (
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\ll 1
). In order for it to be false, the broadcasts of an individual society by itself must be confused in a single observation, not just the population in the entire galaxy. Failure requires extraordinary abundances for the fine observations of individualist surveys, but not so much for the coarse observations used in the collective bound. Combined with the single metasociety assumption, it means that
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
itself is a Poisson point process with distribution

\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}=\frac{d{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}})}\right\rangle}}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}){\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}}\\
\cdot\begin{cases}\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}})&(\text{box})\\
\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\updelta(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}){\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}}&(\text{chord})\end{cases}.

(26)

Finally, in addition to the other assumptions in Table
2
, I assume that the host galaxy
\mathrm{g}
is distant, such that all broadcasts have the same distance
{{d}_{\mathrm{g}}}
and redshift
{{z}_{\mathrm{g}}}
. I even apply this to wide-field surveys of the Milky Way to get order-of-magnitude constraints, as most stars in the Galaxy are of order
\sim 10\ \textrm{kpc}
away.

III
Measurements of aggregate emission

Instruments collect some kind of emission, like energy or photons, and report an observable quantity. The most common kind of observable, one at the heart of most SETI analyses, is the integrated amount of collected emission from a target during a window
\mathsf{x}
. This quantity,
{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}}
for a generic measurable (
{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{x}}}
for collected energy, and
{\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{x}}}
for number of collected photons) has two basic components. First is the aggregate emission from all the broadcasts covered by the observation, denoted
{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
with a superscript
\mathrm{B}
to indicate its origin in broadcasts. Second is the background
{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathfrak{n}}}
, from everything else – instrumental noise, natural background radiation from Earth, the target system, and things behind it.

The mean collected emission of a broadcast is directly proportional to the amount of effective isotropic emission it releases into the universe during the window
{\mathsf{x}}
,
{\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}
. By the time it gets to Earth, propagating an emission distance
{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}
, the emission is “diluted” by a factor
{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}\equiv 1/(4\pi{{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{2})
from the inverse square law. I use the dilution factor in this work because it is more natural when calculating averages, with
\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\propto\left\langle{{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{-2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
occurring frequently.

Extinction can also suppress the observed flux. The surviving fraction of emission in
\mathsf{x}
after extinction is the transmittance
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. Generally, it can be a function of frequency; the transmittance actually depends on the window chosen for wideband broadcasts (e.g., an optical pulse observed with a blue filter will be more subject to dust extinction than one observed with a red filter). Extinction is important for optical light broadcasts more than about a kiloparsec away
[
68
]
; it could also be significant for more exotic bands at the far ends of the electromagnetic spectrum. Extinction is negligible in radio SETI bands (
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\approx 1
). It also includes extinction in the Earth’s atmosphere, which would set
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\approx 0
in much of the infrared spectrum.

The fluence of broadcast
\mathrm{B}
is the amount of emission received per unit area over the window:

{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}=\frac{{\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}}{4\pi{{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{2}}={\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}.

(27)

The emission is collected by an instrument with effective area
\mathcal{A}
and weighted by a nonconstant response function of frequency, time, polarization, and location on the sky. I assume that the response function is
0
outside the times, frequencies, and polarizations covered by
{\mathsf{x}}
, and otherwise only depends on sky location
\boldsymbol{\theta}
:

{\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\theta})={\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot\mathbb{I}[0\leq t-{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}}\leq{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}]\\
\cdot\mathbb{I}[|\nu-\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}|\leq{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}/2]\cdot\mathbb{I}[p\in{\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}]\mathchar 59\relax

(28)

where
\max\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}=1
. Given a fixed broadcast sample, the mean amount of emission measured by the instrument is

{\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle}=\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathfrak{n}}}\right\rangle+{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle={\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}}\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle.

(29)

On the right, the noise is regarded as a virtual “broadcast”
\mathfrak{n}
, with a dummy broadcast tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Calculations are simplified by regarding the emission coming from an “adjoined sample” with this virtual broadcast,
\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}=\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\cup\{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathfrak{n}}\}
.

Of course, we do not know what kinds of broadcasts are present in the sample, so we want to know the statistics of an observable over all possible samples. Under standard assumptions (section
II.7
), the mean intercepted emission within window
\mathsf{x}
is given by

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}.

(30)

This also uses a weighted number of broadcasts,

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}}\equiv{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}})^{n}

(31)

with means

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}}\right\rangle\equiv{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\int_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mathsf{x}}}\frac{d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{d\boldsymbol{\theta}}[\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]^{n}d\boldsymbol{\theta}

(32)

for different exponents
n
(Appendix
A
). In turn,
{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}
is the mean instantaneous number of societies per star,
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
is the mean number of selected broadcasts per society, and
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}
is the number of stars in the window.

According to the law of total variance (Paper I;
Wasserman 140
), the variance in the intercepted emission

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}\right]=\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]+\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]\right\rangle

(33)

splits into a sample variance term and a noise variance term, respectively. Here
\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle
and
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]
refer to the mean and variance, respectively, for a fixed sample from selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{M}})
3
3

3

Actually, the selection associated with
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}
,
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}
, and
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}
is
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{U})
(see Paper I), but all broadcasts are assumed to arise from the single metasociety
\mathrm{M}
in this paper.
, over all possible realizations of the measurement noise. The sample variance for observations of a distant metasociety is then

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]\approx\mathcal{A}^{2}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right.}\\
{\left.+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right)}

(34)

in single metasociety scenarios.

IV
Individualist constraints: Signal-to-noise ratio and confusion

SETI surveys – and indeed most surveys for astrophysical objects – take an individualist approach, looking for single events that stand out from the background with high signal-to-noise ratio. Individualist constraints use the
extreme
values of an observable
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
, but they disregard the rest of its distribution. They can rule out even a single very bright broadcast, but say nothing about even a vast number of very faint broadcasts. In this section, I calculate effective signal-to-noise ratios using the statistics of energy and photon measurements. This lets me explore the effects of confusion with the framework.

IV.1
The effective number of independent measurements

Surveys are groups of observations. An archetypal survey consists of
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\uppi}
nonoverlapping pointings (
\uppi
), each covering a fixed (possibly noncontiguous) field on the sky. While aimed at one location on the sky, the instrument can make independent observations in
\mathsf{N}_{\uppi}^{\uptheta}
resolution elements (
\uptheta_{i}
) on the sky. The sample of stars covered by these resolution elements is essentially fixed even at different times or frequencies. The number of stars covered in the survey is simply the sum over all resolution elements and pointings, and

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\approx\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\uppi}\mathsf{N}_{\uppi}^{\uptheta}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\uptheta_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}}.

(35)

Unless a very bright source spills into multiple resolution elements, measurements made in different resolution elements and pointings are independent, taking samples of different societies.

In the box and chord models, a survey has
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\uppi}\mathsf{N}_{\uppi}^{\uptheta}\mathsf{T}_{\uptheta}\mathsf{B}_{\uptheta}/(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})
observations, but their broadcast samples are not necessarily independent. If broadcasts are very wideband, the same ones will be present across many adjacent frequency channels; very long lived broadcasts will be present in many sequential snapshots. These measurements have high sample covariance, missing the sampling fluctuations between them. I estimate the effective number of independent measurements as
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{eff}}\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle/\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
:

\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{eff}}\approx\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\uppi}\mathsf{N}_{\uppi}^{\uptheta}\frac{(\mathsf{T}_{\uptheta}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\uptheta}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}{(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}&\text{(Box)}\\
\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\uppi}\mathsf{N}_{\uppi}^{\uptheta}\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\uptheta}+\mathsf{T}_{\uptheta}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}&\text{(Chord)}.\end{cases}

(36)

IV.2
Signal-to-noise ratio definitions

Most SETI surveys identify candidate “hits” with a differential measurement, looking for abnormally large fluctuations in measurements that are not expected statistically from noise. The signal-to-noise ratio is thus a key quantity in estimating the sensitivity reach of a SETI program. One can estimate the baseline value of an observable and the usual size of the fluctuations empirically from a collection of related observations. The group of observations analyzed to yield these estimators is not necessarily the survey as a whole, or even all the observations of a particular pointing. Instead, the idea is generally that the observations within a group are comparable, with statistically interchangeable distributions of background (and presumably broadcasts). Hence, we generally want to compare only observations covering the same field at the same time; the noise can change at different times and at different points in the sky. Breakthrough Listen’s
turboSETI
estimates these quantities in narrowband line searches by comparison with other fine channels in a coarse channel, for instance
[
44
]
. The estimation in other programs, particularly those searching for other types of signal, could use different groupings of observations;
\upgamma(\mathsf{o})
stands in for the actual group of observations used.

The signal-to-noise test statistic for each measurement is

{\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]}={\frac{\text{deviation from mean}}{\text{typical fluctuation}}}={\frac{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}-{{\widehat{\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle}_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}}{\displaystyle{{\widehat{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(37)

where
{{\widehat{\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle}_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}
and
{{\widehat{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}
are estimators for the mean and standard deviation of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
respectively. Although the sample mean and sample standard deviation of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
within
\upgamma(\mathsf{o})
might be used to evaluate equation
37
, this is not necessary: in
Enriquez et al. [44]
, for example, the trimmed sample mean and standard deviation are used to discard spectral features induced by the processing. The most extreme values of the sample are excluded (those outside the 5th–95th percentile range in
Enriquez et al. 44
) before the sample mean and standard deviation are calculated
[
125
,
21
, see]
. This makes them more robust to the outliers that are inevitable artifacts of data reduction.

A candidate detection is found for observation
\mathsf{o}
if
\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]>\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
, where the signal-to-noise threshold
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
is calculated to have a negligible false-alarm rate over the entire survey
\mathsf{s}
. A null result happens when

{\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]\right]_{\mathsf{s}}=\mathrm{max}\left[\frac{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}-{{\widehat{\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle}_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}}{\displaystyle{{\widehat{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]_{\upgamma(\mathsf{o})}}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\leq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}.

(38)

We calculate the expected sensitivity of a survey by estimating the maximum
\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]
expected from a population and comparing it to
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
. If the latter is greater, then no detections are expected. Now, the actual value of
\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]
is a nonlinear combination of several factors that is not easily tractable. If different observations have wildly different broadcast populations or noise properties, then the estimated variance itself can vary a lot; the signal-to-noise will be greater in those groups of observations where it is small. A full accounting of these effects may best be found through numerical simulation.

Our goal here is to understand the basic behavior of signal-to-noise ratio and survey sensitivity, how it rises and falls as the number of broadcasts increases. For that reason, I assume that all observations of a metasociety being considered are interchangeable: the
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}}
for each observation
\mathsf{o}_{i}
all have the same statistical properties, with a constant background noise level and similar broadcast populations sampled by each. I also posit that the background noise fluctuations in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
are negligible compared to the contribution of a detectable broadcast. In other words, the large fluctuations that result in a detection happen because the observation covers more broadcasts or brighter broadcasts than typical. The maximum signal-to-noise ratio is then estimated:

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]{=\frac{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};[\mathsf{s}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle}{{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{M}]}\right]}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(39)

with
\mathsf{o}
understood to stand in for any representative observation of the galaxy, because the observations are assumed to be interchangeable.
4
4

4

T
The inverted hat is used here to mean a theoretical estimate, in contrast to an estimator derived from actual data.
This estimate should be adequate as long as the variance between observations is not too great.

This lends itself to two simple approximations for different
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
distributions, applying if the expected numbers of covered broadcasts and societies,
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle
, are
\gtrsim 1
. If the fluence distribution is narrow, then fluctuations in the brightness are due mainly to variability in the number of broadcasts intercepted by a broadcast:

{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}^{[\mathsf{s}]}\right\rangle}\\
\approx{\left(\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\right)}\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle.

(40)

So the biggest signal is the greatest expected excess in the number of broadcasts times the expected emission from a single broadcast. Appendix
D
presents approximations for the median excess number of broadcasts, but when
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim 1
, it has a
\sim\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle^{{1/2}}
dependence up to a logarithmic factor. However, the variance grows at least as quickly, forcing the signal-to-noise ratio to remain below the threshold for detection.

In a broad fluence distribution, however, the fluctuations are determined by a single broadcast that dominates all the others, with
{\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\approx\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}
:

{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle\approx\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}\right]}.

(41)

Power-law distributions (
{\uppsi\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}}\propto\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{-{\mathnormal{\gamma}}}
) behave like broad distributions when
{\mathnormal{\gamma}}<3
, as long as
\mathbb{M}\left[{\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}}\right]
is much smaller than the maximum possible
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
. Note, however, that any distribution with
{\mathnormal{\gamma}}\leq 1
must have a maximum cutoff that is saturated quickly.

I define a single variable, an effective number of broadcasts, to encapsulate both approximations:

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}\equiv\max\left[\left(\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\right)\mathchar 59\relax\right.}\\
{\left.\frac{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}\right]}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle}\right]}\mathchar 59\relax

(42)

with

{\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]\approx\frac{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle}{{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}^{[\mathsf{s}]}\right]}}}.

(43)

Ideally, we would like a very large number of
independent
measurements to both accurately estimate the mean and observe rare maxima in
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
. As noted, wideband and long-duration broadcasts reduce the number of effective independent measurements to
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{eff}}
(equation
36
).

IV.3
The sparse limit

The common assumption in SETI is that broadcasts are very rare, with
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\ll 1
. In the sparse limit, if any broadcasts are intercepted at all, all observations have at most one (
\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}=1
) and the maximum signal-to-noise occurs for the observation containing the brightest broadcast (
\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}=\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}
). Then, a null result implies either

{\frac{\mathrm{max}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}}{{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}}\lesssim\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}~\mathrm{or}~{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=0:

(44)

they are either too faint or too rare to observe. The two conditions in fact correspond to the luminosity and rate limits that are so commonly quoted in SETI (e.g.,
Enriquez et al. 44
and references therein, as shown in that work’s Figure 7).

Usually, the broadcasts are assumed to be sparse enough that the background noise is expected to dominate the variance, with
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right]
used to estimate total variance. Sufficiently powerful broadcasts can dominate the variance, however, long before
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
increases past
1
, just because they are contributing so much energy. Trimmed means and standard deviations help address this problem by discarding a certain percentage of the observations with the highest values of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}
, but once
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle
rises past that fraction, the effect of broadcasts on the noise must be taken into account.

IV.4
The confusion limit

As
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle
increases past
1
, however,
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
nearly converges to a stable distribution by the central limit theorem under certain general conditions
[
43
]
.
5
5

5

T
These conditions do not precisely hold because
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is itself a random variable; the limiting distribution is a mixture of stable distributions. Nonetheless, in the diffuse approximation for Poissonian broadcasts,
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
itself converges to a narrow Gaussian, so the effects of the spread are weak.
If
\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
exists, then once there are enough broadcasts to sample the emission distribution well in each observation (
\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\approx\left\langle{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
),
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
should approach
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
by the law of large numbers. This is a quantity that continues growing with
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
. The mean emission from all the broadcasts intercepted by an observation eventually becomes much greater than the brightest single broadcast. In this confusion limit, the broadcasts effectively blend together into another noise background and the individualist approach of equation
38
fails. This leads to a new problem in SETI: we might fail to detect ETIs not because they are too rare but because they are too common!

To be clear, confusion only sets in when there are too many broadcasts per observation – generally a separate epoch, channel, and beam in radio. The number of broadcasts covered in a survey, or even a single pointing, can be far greater, while still leaving “empty” observations that contrast with the occupied observations. The more fine-grained the survey, the less of an issue confusion should be. Modern surveys may cover billions of observations and could detect many millions of broadcasts before confusion sets in.
