The convergence happens even in the absence of noise and is an effect of the underlying distribution of samples. Confusion is implicit in the sample variance
\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]
. Because the variance is necessarily larger than the sample variance alone, equation
34
gives a hard upper bound on the estimated signal-to-noise ratio:

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}\left[\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle\frac{\left\langle{{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right]^{-1/2}

(45)

for distant galaxies with one metasociety under the diffuse approximation. Confusion resulting from the sample variance alone is dubbed here “sample confusion”, to contrast with the (rarer) “noise confusion” from noise variance. Sample confusion can be said to set in when the right-hand side falls below the detection threshold
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
.

Assuming that the fluence distribution is narrow and the diffuse approximation applies, this converges to

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim\left(\mathbb{M}\left[{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}\right]-{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle}\right)/\sqrt{{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}}{.}

(46)

The sample variance is driven by a kind of shot noise – not in photons or electrons, but in the broadcasts themselves, akin to the “graininess” of good optical images of galaxies arising from variations in the number of discrete bright stars
[
136
]
. But the numerator falls and rises as
\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]/\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
; this ratio is large only if most observations have no broadcasts so we can compare unoccupied and occupied observations. Thus, the estimated maximum signal-to-noise ratio decreases at least as
{\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle^{1/2}}
, until
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim{1/\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}}
by which point it is too small to pass the stringent cuts used to eliminate false positives.

Broad
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
distributions may be essentially immune to confusion even when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\gg 1
, because increasing
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
also results in much brighter broadcasts being intercepted. The maximum fluence broadcast rises above the background of confused broadcasts present in all observations for a broad enough distribution. Often, however, there is a maximum fluence, limited by the maximum available power, the nearest star, or zero drift rate. These brightest broadcasts must be submerged in the aggregate background in order for confusion to set in. Define a window
\upvarsigma
that samples from
\mathsf{o}
but only passes broadcasts from this end of the distribution:

\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\upvarsigma\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}=\{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}~{and}~\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\approx\max\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\}.

(47)

This effectively
induces
a narrow fluence distribution in the remaining sample; if there’s already a narrow fluence distribution, the
\upvarsigma
window is equivalent to the observation window. Now, if the bright subset of broadcasts picked by
\upvarsigma
are confused on their own, then the entire sample is confused – there are no even brighter broadcasts to stick out of the blended emission, and the faint broadcasts only add to the “noise.” Thus, a very conservative constraint for confusion is that it sets in when

{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upvarsigma\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}\gtrsim 1/{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}^{2}{\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(48)

an approximate result for equation
46
applied to a population of only these brightest broadcasts, with the signal-to-noise required to be above
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
.

IV.5
The rise and fall of signal-to-noise ratio

Consider the behavior of
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
when
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
has a narrow distribution. As
{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}
increases, we can discern six regimes:

•

In a null regime,
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}=0
. Derived signal-to-noise ratios have values of order unity, resulting entirely from fluctuations in the background.

•

When considering possible hits, most SETI analyses work in a strongly sparse regime, where the broadcasts are rare enough to have no effect on the estimated background. Furthermore,
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}=1
because there are too few observations for multiple broadcasts to ever “touch” the same observation window. Thus,
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
remains constant at a value that can be
\gg 1
.

•

In a moderately sparse regime, the background noise continues to dominate the variance. Although the typical observation still has no broadcasts, some have at least one, and
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}>1
.
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
is rising in this regime because
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}
is growing while
{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}
remains below one. The onset of this regime occurs at
{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}\sim{(\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{eff}})^{-1/2}}
.

•

In a transition regime, the broadcasts themselves dominate the variance of
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}
. The slow rise of
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}}
is overcome by the rising variance.
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
falls as
{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle^{-1/2}}
to
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle^{-1}
in this regime, although it may be still high enough to claim a detection. Using the trimmed means and standard deviations effectively delays the onset of the transition regime so that it has no dependence on
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{eff}}
.

•

In the confusion regime, the typical observation has at least one broadcast.
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
continues to decline due to the sample variance, as according to equation
45
. The rate of the decline slows down because
{\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}
starts growing as
{{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}}}}^{1/2}
. At this point
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
is a factor of order unity, too small to make any individualist detections.

•

Finally, radio broadcasts experience a mutual interference regime, where the broadcasts are so numerous that the noise variance from their wave noise dominates over the sample variance.
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
becomes indistinguishable from the null case. Equation
39
no longer describes the numerator of
\mathfrak{S}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
– the wave noise fluctuations from the interference between the broadcasts, not the Poisson fluctuations, dominate variations in
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}
.

In short, the signal-to-noise ratio at first rises as broadcasts start being sampled and then falls as they overlap with each other. The borders between these regimes depend on the number of independent observations, the construction of observations, and the luminosities of the broadcasts. Not all of these regimes occur for all parameters or all types of broadcasts. In broad distributions,
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
is governed by the broadcasts with the greatest fluence. The strong sparse regime can split into two stages: signal-to-noise ratio increases at first, as signals with increasing energy per observation are intercepted, followed by a regime of flat signal-to-noise ratio, where all observations have 0 or 1 broadcast with near-maximal fluence.

V
Individualist constraints: radio broadcasts

V.1
Wave noise, modes, and amplitudes

Natural electromagnetic radiation can be regarded as the sum of many microscopic emitters with different locations, frequencies, and phases. The mutual interference between all these sources results in the amplitude of the detected radiation fluctuating chaotically instead of maintaining a constant magnitude. In quantum terms, chaotic light displays photon bunching
[
46
,
132
,
153
, e.g.,]
. These wave noise fluctuations greatly dominate over photon shot noise at radio wavelengths because the photon occupation number in each field mode (cell in phase space) is much greater than one
[
108
]
. Thermal noise in the receiver usually overwhelms over the wave noise from sources, with a greater noise temperature, but it too is the chaotic sum of many microscopic fluctuations. Artificial broadcasts may be coherent (
Hippke 64
; see also Appendix B of
Cordes et al. 34
), but wave noise is still present in the background and in the mutual interference of many broadcasts.

Although we generally measure energy in radio SETI, the statistics of the intercepted energy follow from the underlying wave amplitudes of the electric field, commonly measured as voltages
[
142
]
. Each amplitude is measured for an individual mode
\upmu
of the electromagnetic field. A mode is an independent oscillator of the electromagnetic field. A photon can be localized to a single mode, but no further. In terms of temporal properties, a mode includes only one polarization and has a bandwidth-duration product
\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}\mathsf{B}_{\upmu}=1

[
148
,
101
,
22
,
64
]
. Amplitudes add linearly: the amplitude measured in mode
\upmu
is

\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}=\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}.

(49)

The background amplitude
\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}
is an independent random variable with a zero-mean complex Gaussian distribution
[
142
, e.g.,]
. The same is true for the amplitudes of broadcasts that are incoherent, although each has its own amplitude variance.

Square-law detectors derive the energy in the mode by taking the square of its complex modulus,
\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}={\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}}{{\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}}}^{\ast}
.
6
6

6

This is the power if voltage is being directly squared, up to a constant factor; I adopt a convention that
|\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}|=\sqrt{\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}}
.
As a thermal noise, the background energy
\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}=|{\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}}|^{2}
per mode has a mean value
k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}
, which is the background temperature (including system noise and natural background radio flux in the field) multiplied by Boltzmann’s constant (
Radhakrishnan 108
).
7
7

7

T
This also follows from the Rayleigh-Jeans law, with one mode covering a solid angle equal to the wavelength squared divided by collecting area
[
152
]
.
The energy intercepted from a broadcast,
{\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}
, is the product of fluence, collecting area, and instrumental response.
8
8

8

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}
is the
energy
(power) beam pattern of the instrument, not the amplitude (voltage) response
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{A};\upmu}=\sqrt{\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}}
.
Thus,

\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}({\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}}){\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(50)

and, for distant metasocieties,

\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}\right\rangle\approx k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}.

(51)

The variance depends on the number and type of broadcasts.

V.2
Energy measured in an observation

Where complications arise is that usually the analysis does not work directly with these mode energies either. The energy in
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}
modes per observation – covering different times, frequencies, or polarizations – is summed together to yield an observed energy
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}
:

\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}=\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}\mathchar 59\relax

(52)

where
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}
is the set of modes that are summed for the observation
\mathsf{o}
. It can be assumed that the modes are interchangeable, with statistically equivalent populations of broadcasts, because observations are so fine-grained. The mean energy in the observation follows simply enough from equation
51
and the linearity of expectation:

\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle\approx\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

(53)

assuming that the target galaxy is distant.

The sample variance for a distant galaxy with a single metasociety is

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]\approx\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle\left[{\left\langle{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right.\\
\left.{+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right].

(54)

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
is the mean number of broadcasts per society, representing a clumping effect.

In Appendix
B.1
, I show that the noise variance for distant metasocieties is

\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]\right\rangle\approx\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]}\\
+\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right].

(55)

by working with the amplitudes. The covariance
\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]
for broadcast
\mathrm{B}
equals
\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
and is related to the coherence properties of the broadcast.

The noise variance includes the mutual interference of the background and numerous broadcasts, resulting in a quasi-thermal background even if the broadcasts are coherent. This interference puts an additional upper limit to the signal-of-noise ratio of

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim\frac{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}.

(56)

This can lead to a “noise confusion”, which in some cases can set in before the previously derived sample confusion (equation
46
).

V.3
Incoherent radio broadcasts

The incoherent case applies to all known natural phenomena, including astrophysical masers where radio amplitude statistics have been measured
[
45
]
. It may also apply to artificial broadcasts if they are sufficiently broadband, as expected if they are rich in information
[
22
,
96
, see]
. We can model a broadcast as a burst of white noise with a (possibly frequency drifting) bandpass filter applied to it, as in the box model; if the instantaneous bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
is wider than
\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}^{-1}
, the broadcast is incoherent. Furthermore, when many coherent broadcasts are confused, the aggregate emission also behaves like an incoherent source.

The noise variance calculation is presented in Appendix
B.1
, but essentially it may be calculated from the fact that the noise is independent between modes. We find for distant galaxies under the diffuse approximation

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]\approx\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}\\
+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left[\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}+\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right]

(57)

(see Appendix
B.2
for the full expression).

V.3.1
Confusion and searching for continuum sources in the box model

In the box model, hisses are flat-spectrum continuum sources (
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
,
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
). When observing a distant galaxy in both polarizations,

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]\approx{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left[\left(1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle\frac{{\left\langle(\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}+1\right)\right]^{-1/2}

(58)

where I have applied the diffuse approximation, and

\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\equiv\frac{\mathcal{A}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}}\approx\frac{\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}{{2}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}}

(59)

is the expected signal-to-noise ratio for a single broadcast at
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\mathsf{o}}=1
in the presence of background noise only. If individual broadcasts in isolation are detectable (
\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\gtrsim 1
), the threshold of the transition region (section
IV.5
) is passed when
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim 1/\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}
. The confusion regime occurs around when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim 1/\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}
, which translates to

\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\right]^{-1}

(60)

for hisses. Broadcast mutual interference becomes the dominant noise source for
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}
. Figure
2
shows how
{\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]}
varies with
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle
under standard assumptions – in particular, how the transition regime is absent for
\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]=1
, while the moderate sparse regime vanishes for high
\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]
.

Figure 2:
Growth and fall of total signal-to-noise ratio
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
as the number of broadcasts per observation increases and confusion sets in, as depicted for for radio continuum sources, in the hiss regime of the box model. I adopt the standard assumptions of Table
2
. All broadcasts have the same luminosity. Each individual source, in the absence of other sources, would have a signal-to-noise ratio of
\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]
ranging from
1
to
1000
. The blue line indicates the bound from sample variance. The mutual interference regime, where the approximation for
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
breaks down, is shaded in grey. The survey contains
10^{6}
independent observations, where each observation is summed from
10^{3}
electromagnetic modes.

V.4
Fully and partially coherent radio broadcasts

Unlike known natural sources, artificial radio broadcasts can be coherent. Although a perfect coherent carrier contains negligible information, it conveys one important fact very well – the existence of technology at that location – perhaps “advertising” dimmer information-rich broadcasts in the vicinity.

Perfectly coherent broadcasts are characterized as perfect chirps, with a constant luminosity and a well-defined phase
\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}
at any time:
{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{B}}}(t{\mathchar 59\relax p})=|{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{B}}(p)}|\exp(i{\varphi_{\mathrm{B}}}(t{\mathchar 59\relax p}))
. The measured amplitudes can vary from mode to mode as the broadcast drifts across channels, but there is zero noise variance, ignoring the miniscule Poissonian photon shot noise –
{\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{B}}}
is exactly proportional to
{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathrm{B}}}
– and thus all covariance terms are zero.

The partially coherent case is an important one for actual ultranarrowband broadcasts. Any modulation of a perfect chirp will broaden the instantaneous bandwidth. This leads to fluctuations that are correlated on long timescales. In fact, even if the broadcast itself is perfectly coherent, scintillation in the interstellar medium introduces observed variability (
Cordes et al. 34
;
Brzycki et al. 17
). In the partially coherent case, the coherence timescale
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}\approx{\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}^{-1}}
is greater than
\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}
. Modes of the same polarization and separated by less than
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}
in time then have
{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]\sim\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}}
. The fall-off of the covariance can be described by the well-known
{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(\Updelta t)
function (e.g.,
Foellmi 46
; see Appendix
B.3
).

Both the fully coherent and partially coherent cases can be covered in a single formula with the use of a factor describing the self-interference:

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]\approx\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}\\
+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle{\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})

(61)

in the diffuse approximation for a distant galaxy. The coherence term
{\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}
ranges from
0
for fully coherent broadcasts to
1
for polarized, partially coherent broadcasts with slow (
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
), high-amplitude fluctuations (see Appendix
B.1
).

Although the variance is missing a term related to self-interference, it is greatly increased by strong, long-timescale fluctuations, with
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\approx 1
essentially doubling the sample variance. It may seem odd that a perfectly coherent broadcast with
{\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}}=0
has less noise variance than expected from Gaussian statistics, while a partially coherent broadcast with
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}
can have more, despite being indistinguishable – especially since this is the regime of actual coherent broadcasts from space modulated by strong scattering. The discrepancy basically amounts to whether we treat the fluctuations as noise variance or sample variance.

Suppose we took a snapshot of a large population of partially coherent broadcasts, all with infinite life spans and fully polarized, all with the same
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
, and all with the same
time-averaged
luminosity. Because they are only partially coherent, the
measured
brightness of the different broadcasts will vary – in fact, we might generally expect them to have an exponential distribution just as if they are incoherent
[
34
, compare with]
.
9
9

9

The wave noise of an unpolarized broadcast does not have an exponential distribution – the power received in
each
polarization has an exponential distribution, but the polarizations can vary independently of each other. As a result, the total power in both polarizations has a
\upchi^{2}_{4}
distribution. An exponential distribution still applies if the modulation in both polarizations is identical, as might apply for interstellar scintillation.
The partial coherence approach treats the variations as the result of microscopic fluctuations on an underlying constant luminosity. Thus, the variations are included in the noise variance.

But since the fluctuations are much too slow to observe, from an empirical point of view, we could conclude that the broadcasts are perfectly coherent but that
the luminosities themselves differ
. That is, the exponential distribution in fluence reflects an exponential distribution in luminosity, and thus we regard the fluctuations as sample variance, resulting from a
{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}=2{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}^{2}
term. Either approach is consistent as long as we choose one convention and stick with it, to avoid double-counting the fluctuations.

If we take many snapshots of the population separated by
\gg{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}
, we will observe the partially coherent broadcasts varying in luminosity. The sample variance approach would interpret this as intrinsic variability in the broadcast luminosities themselves; the instantaneous distribution of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
in the population would reflect the temporal distribution of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
for each individual broadcast. Since the box and chord models assume a nonvarying luminosity, I proceed with the noise interpretation for partially coherent broadcasts (degenerate
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=1
). Despite this, I group the corresponding variance term with the sample variance because it behaves in exactly the same way, considering this noise term to contribute to sample confusion instead of noise confusion.

V.4.1
Confusion and lines in the chord model

Ultranarrowband line searches look for lines with different drift rates, essentially seeking concentrations of energy when summing along skewed lines in spectrograms (for example, by applying a frequency shift to each time step, as in
Siemion et al. 119
). The dedrifting is performed on data that sample time and frequency (
\mathsf{d}
-type windows). With coherent dedispersion, each data point corresponds to one mode. More often, dedrifting is performed on a spectrogram where each data point is the sum of the energy in several modes, summed sequentially in time
[
85
, as in]
. After dedrifting, a detected line appears on a spectrogram like a broadened line with no drift. An observation is constructed by summing several sequential data points together into an observation. Each observation has an associated (de)drift rate
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
applied to it. A dedrifted line
\mathrm{B}
has

{\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}(\text{dedrifted})\right\rangle}\approx\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\mathsf{o}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}})\mathcal{A}{\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\max(1\mathchar 59\relax{|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})}.

(62)

The second term in the denominator accounts for smearing resulting from the line crossing in and out of each datum point within
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}

[
117
,
94
]
. Any remaining lines continue to have a range of drift rates.

In order to understand how a wider spread in drift rate affects detectability, I express the signal-to-noise of an observation in terms of the counterfactual expected number of broadcasts and individual broadcast signal-to-noise ratio if the drift rate for all broadcasts were forced to zero. The former quantity is

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}

(63)

(equation
21
), and is the expected number of broadcasts per observation channel at any one instant. It is a direct proxy for the abundance of transmitters. The latter is

\displaystyle\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]

\displaystyle\equiv\check{\mathfrak{s}}[{\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}={\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=}0}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{A}{\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}{{2}\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}}.

(64)

There are fewer broadcasts in the absence of drift, but they individually would have higher signal-to-noise ratios, and the product is invariant (
\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
; see Appendix
B.3.1
).

For a randomly chosen trial drift rate
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
, there may be lines that serendipitously are dedrifted, but a SETI analysis seeks for drift rates that yield the highest signal-to-noise ratio. Hence, sensitivity is generally better evaluated by supposing that the analysis has found one of these lines and chosen
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
for that line. We specify whether or not we have found a line with
\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
: it is
1
when we consider only an observation that definitely has a dedrifted line in it, and
0
if we have a typical observation with a typical sample of serendipitous lines. For observations in both polarizations of a distant galaxy with transmitters of identical luminosity, the diffuse approximation gives us

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]\approx\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left[\frac{{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}{\max(1\mathchar 59\relax{|}\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}{|}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})}+\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}\right]\\
{\cdot}\left[\left(1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.+\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})\right]^{-1/2}\mathchar 59\relax

(65)

where
{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}/(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}})}
and
\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}
is a quantity describing the contribution of serendipitous lines (see Appendix
B.3.4
for details). The value of
\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
is difficult to calculate when a dedrifting algorithm is applied, because of the way it “slices” lines, complicating the analysis (Appendix
B.3.1
). I present results for the case when no dedrifting is applied in Figure
3
, in which case there is no smearing. Basically, however, the signal-to-noise ratio for high intrinsic drift rate lines is expected to be suppressed, but it may be more resistant to confusion.

Figure 3:
The impact of confusion on
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]
, the estimated maximum signal-to-noise ratio found in the survey, when observing a population of narrowband lines, as compared for different drift rate distributions. I adopt the standard assumptions of Table
2
. The lines all have the same luminosity; individually they would have signal-to-noise 1000 if they were not drifting (
\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]={1000}
). Their drift rate distribution has a spread of
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}=\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
, centered on
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}=0
. No dedrifting has been applied (
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
). The blue lines show the limits from sample variance alone, and determines when sample confusion occurs. The red dotted lines show limits from the quasi-thermal noise variance caused by the mutual interference and self-noise of all the broadcasts and background, and determines when noise confusion occurs. Wider spreads in drift rates delay the onset of sample confusion, but not noise confusion. Different line styles indicate whether or not we conditionalize on having found a line: solid for
{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=1
(a line has definitely been found), and dashed for
{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=0
(only serendipitous lines are included). The observations here are modeled according to the high spectral resolution filterbank products used in Breakthrough Listen for the Green Bank Telescope, for a single coarse channel (two polarizations,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}=51\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}
,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}=16\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
,
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{s}}=2^{20}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
).

Dedrifting serves to accumulate the energy in
\max[1\mathchar 59\relax{|}\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}{|}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}]
channels when we do find a line (
{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=1
). Sample variance is generally suppressed; when
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
,
{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\propto{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}}^{-1}\ln{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}}
– the mean collected fluence is the same, but it is divided among a larger number of quickly transiting broadcasts. In the sparse regime, the smearing within each time point decreases the signal-to-noise ratio of individual broadcasts
[
117
,
94
, as noted in]
. The reduction of sample variance can be expected to delay sample confusion, however, compared to the low drift rate case.

We can evaluate when sample confusion sets in by again using the sample variance as a minimum variance and finding when
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}
. The sample variance is increased by a factor
(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})
because the slow wave noise fluctuations effectively spread out the intrinsic luminosity distribution (section
V.4
). As discussed in Appendix
B.3.5
, confusion is evaluated for
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
, because some lines will have nearly zero drift rate by chance, and these, with minimal leakage into other channels, will be the last to be confused as
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
increases. Using the numerator from equation
65
to define the expected maximum signal, detection fails when

\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}\gtrsim\frac{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}+\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}}{\sqrt{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})}}\mathchar 59\relax

(66)

with the standard assumptions and when all broadcasts have the same luminosity. Let us focus on the signal from a single detected nondrifting line (
\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}=1
,
\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}=0
). By solving for
\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\approx\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
, I find that sample confusion results in a null detection when

{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\gtrsim\left[{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}^{2}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})\right]^{-1}

(67)

When the drift rate spread becomes big enough, most lines are spread over many observations, and sample variance is so low that noise confusion becomes the impediment to detection when there are many broadcasts. This is seen in Figure
3
for the highest
\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{M}}(\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})
cases. Using the quasi-thermal noise term in the variance, and again evaluating when no dedrifting is applied with standard assumptions, detection fails when

\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}\lesssim\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\frac{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}+\Updelta\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}.

(68)

I again solve for
\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\approx\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
, finding that noise confusion prevents a detection for

\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]^{-1}.

(69)

Since most radio SETI observations use filterbank products that integrate many modes together, noise confusion is only a concern when the drift rate spread is quite high. For a zero-centered drift rate distribution, noise confusion happens first if
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}/\ln(\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})\gtrsim\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}(1+\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}})\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
, which I estimate to be
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\gtrsim 14\ \textrm{Hz}\,\textrm{s}^{-1}
for line searches with the Green Bank Telescope.

V.5
Special considerations for radio interferometers

Interferometric arrays are playing an increasing role in radio SETI
[
110
,
58
,
137
, e.g.,]
. Nominally, they provide increased angular resolution, which should decrease
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}
,
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
, and thus confusion. But the situation is complicated by the presence of strong sidelobes in the synthesized “dirty” beam that span the primary (antenna) beam.

Signals from the
\mathcal{N}_{A}
antennas in an array are combined either coherently or incoherently. Incoherent summing simply adds together the voltages from all the antennas. The summed beam pattern is the same as the primary beam pattern, an advantage when searching for bright signals over a wide field. Sensitivity increases as
\mathcal{N}_{A}^{1/2}
if detector noise dominates the variance in
\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}
, but celestial radio noise hampers sensitivity if it dominates the variance
[
77
]
, analogous to the self-noise limit for single-dish observations
[
108
]
. Sample confusion occurs when there is one broadcast on average in the time-frequency window of the observation anywhere within the primary beam.

Coherent beamforming processes the voltages to synthesize narrow beams with complex sidelobes. The “thinned array curse” implies that, given a fixed collection of antennas, the maximum gain is invariant with respect to how those antennas are arranged
[
48
]
.
10
10

10

Originally derived for energy transmitters, it also applies to receivers by reciprocity.
Increasing the spacing of the antennas merely shrinks the size of the main lobe. It can be shown then that the mean response over the primary beam is
1/\mathcal{N}_{A}
, and for a sparse array with filling factor
\ll 1
, almost all of this response comes from the sidelobes.

There are two basic strategies for beamforming that are used. Coherent summing synthesizes tied-array beams by applying phase corrections to each antenna before adding the voltages. It requires no time-averaging and is used for observations of transients and pulsars
[
124
]
. It also is the basis of Breakthrough Listen’s million-star survey with MeerKAT
[
35
]
. However, computational limits currently allow only a few dozen beams to be formed in the primary beam, sampling only a small part of it. Aperture synthesis, the more well-known technique, performs a Fourier transform on the visibilities formed by multiplying the voltages of two antennas together and time-averaging. This approach generates a map of the entire primary beam but is not suited for detection of rapid transients because of the averaging and computational cost. In addition, only baselines between distinct antennas are included, so there is no sensitivity to diffuse backgrounds that cover the entire primary beam: these observations only detect spatial
fluctuations
in broadcasts and could fail to detect a heavily populated galaxy covering a large enough sky area.

In both approaches, the dirty beam sidelobes have an amplitude
1/\mathcal{N}_{A}
.
11
11

11

I
In aperture synthesis, the lack of zero-spacing data shifts the beam pattern to have zero mean overall with negative responses over much of the sidelobes
[
76
]
.
The relatively strong sidelobes lead to much greater confusion than would normally be expected, hampering deconvolution of the dirty map. Under most circumstances relevant to radio astronomy, the sky can be regarded as basically empty, with only a few sources covering a small fraction of the primary beam, so this is not an issue (
Högbom 66
). If there are more sources in the primary beam than independent measurements, however, it becomes impossible to disentangle them. Coherent summing yields only one independent measurement per antenna per observation,
\mathcal{N}_{A}
in total; aperture synthesis provides only one per baseline,
\mathcal{N}_{B}
in total
[
66
,
116
]
. Thus, confusion necessarily sets in when

\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}_{\mathrm{A}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle\gtrsim\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}_{A}&(\text{coherent summing})\\
\mathcal{N}_{B}&(\text{aperture synthesis})\end{cases}.

(70)

The minimum number of baselines is
\mathcal{N}_{A}(\mathcal{N}_{A}-1)/2
from a single snapshot. Rotational synthesis using multiple snapshots to increase
\mathcal{N}_{B}
is limited to long-lasting broadcasts.

VI
Individualist constraints: optical broadcasts

VI.1
Noise variance in optical photon counting

At frequencies beyond radio, the mean number of photons arriving in an electromagnetic field mode (the photon occupation number) is much less than
1
. When that happens, the photon shot noise completely overwhelms the photon bunching effect of wave noise
[
108
]
.
12
12

12

Under certain circumstances, photon counts can have
sub-Poissonian
statistics, with the Fock states having no number fluctuations at all
[
46
, e.g.,]
. This is a purely quantum phenomenon, requiring photon detectors to measure, and is not expected from natural astrophysical sources, but it could be a technosignature
[
64
]
.

Instruments at these higher frequencies nowadays count photons, with the measured quantity being the number of photons collected during an observation,
\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}
. The number of photons from the background,
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}
, can be regarded as a pure Poisson random variable. In the absence of modulation, the number of photons from each broadcast
\mathrm{B}
,
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
, is also Poissonian. This greatly simplifies analysis compared to the radio case, because the sum of a fixed number of independent Poisson random variables is another Poisson random variable
[
75
]
. I assume the broadcasts have constant intrinsic luminosity, as in the box model, with no significant modulation on timescales
\gtrsim\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
. Let
{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}
be the photon fluence from each broadcast
{\mathrm{B}}
within the observation
\mathsf{o}
. For a distant metasociety, the mean is

\left\langle\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}.}

(71)

In the absence of attenuation, it can also be shown (Appendix
C
) that the variance under the diffuse approximation is

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]\approx\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}\\
{+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle(\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}^{2}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}\mathchar 59\relax

(72)

which includes the mean number
\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle
of noise photons from the sky background, dark current, and readout noise. Slow modulation increases the variance, which can be modeled either as an intrinsic spread in the photon fluence of the broadcasts or by an additional self-noise term (much like as in Section
V.4
).

VI.2
The behavior of signal-to-noise ratio for Poissonian photon counts

It is straightforward to estimate the signal-to-noise ratio with photon counting instruments at high frequencies using equation
72
. The expected signal-to-noise ratio of an isolated broadcast at
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{Q};\mathsf{o}}=1
,

\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]{\equiv}\frac{\mathcal{A}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{\sqrt{\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}}{=\frac{\mathcal{A}\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\sqrt{\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(73)

provides another convenient scaling variable. Then, for distant galaxies and using the diffuse approximation,

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}]={\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]{\cdot}\left(1+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\frac{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}}\right\rangle}{\sqrt{\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}}\right.\\
\left.+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}}\right\rangle\frac{{\left\langle({\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{{\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}}\right)^{-1/2}.

(74)

Sample variance exceeds background noise when

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle>\left(\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\frac{\left\langle({\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)^{-1}\mathchar 59\relax

(75)

similar to the radio case. Moreover, sample variance dominates the photon shot noise from the broadcasts when

\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}>\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{[\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}]}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)\right]^{-1}.

(76)

Under normal circumstances, this second inequality holds as long as at least
{\sim 1}
photon is expected to be detected per broadcast.

This means that there is no mutual interference regime for optical broadcasts –
\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}]
remains at a factor of a few from sample variance alone. Noise confusion by itself never prevents detection. Because each broadcast is contributing many photons, the photons can be thought of as coming in groups, one for each broadcast. This introduces an intrinsic “graininess” to the photon count statistics, reminiscent of the surface brightness fluctuations observed in distant galaxies from discrete bright stars
[
136
,
109
]
. Now, there still is a (sample) confusion regime, so this cannot be exploited to make an individual detection and does not intrinsically favor optical over radio. Perhaps the graininess might be discerned in the photon count statistics when in the confusion regime, and the lack of interference can extend the range of that kind of technique in optical. In any case, the confusion regime already implies extremely high abundances of broadcasts, so these considerations only apply to an arguably contrived region of parameter space.

VII
The collective bound: The total emission of the galaxy

The collective bound, by contrast, is based on the total emission received from all the broadcasts in the target galaxy. The concept is very simple: the broadcast from ETIs cannot outshine the galaxy as a whole (including the broadcasts) when we’re looking at it. To be clear, this is an entirely empirical constraint, applying the observed emission rather than how much emission we expect from natural processes. It is possible that ETIs might vastly increase the apparent luminosity of a galaxy by harnessing its central black hole or invoking unknown physics – or, more practically, by beaming emission in our direction – but the observed emission necessarily includes this increased luminosity, along with any additional natural emission. We already know that there are no trillion
{L}_{\odot}
radio beacons in M31, for instance, because the radio luminosity of M31 is a lot lower than a trillion Suns, even though such beacons could hypothetically be constructed.

It is impossible to make a detection using the collective approach alone, since any emission we detect could be natural, but an estimated fluence
\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}
derived from measurement does let us conclude that most likely
{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\lesssim\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}
. Statistical analysis lets us constrain a combination of the abundance and brightness distribution.

Observations in the literature effectively report fluences, generally cast as a flux. Now, the variance in
{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
includes sample variance and noise variance. Noise variance is included in the reported errors
\Updelta\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}
on the reported fluence. Given a cumulative fluence distribution
F[\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}]
, a confidence interval only allows those models for which

{\bar{\mathnormal{P}}\leq}~{F[\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}](\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}+C_{\mathsf{o}}\Updelta\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}})}\mathchar 59\relax

(77)

where
{C_{\mathsf{o}}}
is some predefined constant and
\bar{\mathnormal{P}}\approx 0
is a conservative probability threshold. That is, we want to include all models where there is any significant chance that the broadcast population is fainter than the observed luminosity. When only an upper limit on the emission is known, it can be substituted for
\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}+C_{\mathsf{o}}\Updelta\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}
. Taking the inverse CDF of both sides, we find

F^{-1}[\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}](\bar{\mathnormal{P}})\leq\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}+C_{\mathsf{o}}\Updelta\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}.

(78)

If the broadcast fluence has a narrow distribution with a well-defined variance, we can approximate the left-hand side as

F^{-1}[\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}](\bar{\mathnormal{P}})\approx\left\langle{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle-C_{\mathrm{M}}{\mathbb{V}^{1/2}\left[\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]}

(79)

for some suitable constant
C_{\mathrm{M}}
that absorbs the dependence on
\bar{\mathnormal{P}}
.
13
13

13

If the aggregate fluence has a power-law tail, the distribution is likely to be highly asymmetrical and this approximation will fail, even using the regularized mean and variance.

Now, say that
all
of the observed fluence from from broadcasts, with no natural background. We could estimate the number of broadcasts that are contributing to the observed emission simply as

{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=\frac{\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}+C_{\mathsf{o}}\Updelta\widehat{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}}}}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}=\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}(\widehat{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu}}+C_{\mathsf{o}}\Updelta\widehat{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu}})}{\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(80)

the number of typical broadcasts that can “fit” into the emission (in terms of fluence and spectral luminosity, respectively). This naive estimate is in fact central to the collective bound. Under our usual assumptions of a single galactic metasociety, with interchangeable broadcasts and societies and Poissonian
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}
and
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}

\left\langle{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle{\approx}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}

(81)

and

\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]{\approx}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right)

(82)

Plugging these into the left-hand side of equation
79
by use of equation
78
, we find the expression for the collective bound:

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle{\lesssim}{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}+\frac{C^{\prime 2}}{2}\left(1+\sqrt{1+\frac{4}{C^{\prime 2}}{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(83)

where

C^{\prime}\equiv C_{\mathrm{M}}\sqrt{\frac{\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}.

(84)

The meaning of equation
83
depends on the average brightness of a broadcast. When
\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
is very small, many broadcasts can “fit” into the observed emission, and equation
83
reduces to
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\leq{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}+C^{\prime}\sqrt{{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}
, the maximum number that are allowed before the aggregate population outshines the actual emission (with allowances for Poissonian fluctuations).

When
\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
is very bright, even one broadcast would produce more emission than is observed, and is thus ruled out by how faint the galaxy actually is. In this limit, when the broadcast fluence distribution is narrow,
{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\to 0
and equation
83
reduces to
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\lesssim C^{\prime 2}
. When
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\lesssim 1
,
C^{\prime}\approx C_{\mathrm{M}}
, so the bound says that broadcasts are just too rare to have been caught in the observation:
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\lesssim C_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}
. If
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\gtrsim 1
, broadcasts outnumber societies and
C^{\prime}\approx C_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
; equation
83
simplifies to
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\lesssim C_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}
. Equation
83
thus contains the discreteness bound (Paper I) – it cannot rule out a model that predicts a good chance of there being no societies or broadcasts covered by an observation, because no artificial emission is expected then.

In the box model, equations
83
and
17
give us the constraint that either
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\approx 0
or

{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\lesssim\frac{{{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}+(C^{\prime 2}/2)\left[1+\sqrt{1+4{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}/C^{\prime 2}}\right]}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}+{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}{.}

(85)

Applying the collective bound instead to the chord model using equation
21
, the bound is

{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\lesssim\frac{{{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}+(C^{\prime 2}/2)\left[1+\sqrt{1+4{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}/C^{\prime 2}}\right]}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}(1+{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}{)}}

(86)

unless
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\approx 0}
. Observations of continuum galactic luminosity use wideband observations, so
(1+\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})\approx 1
in practice.

The collective bound is sensitive to the full brightness distribution instead of only the most extreme values, and thus explores a different range of parameter space than individualist searches. Observations used to derive galactic luminosities are usually relatively wideband by SETI standards; they also have long integration times compared to the time resolution of pulse searches. This increases the fluence collected from noise and background emission, burying the brightest individual signals, but the mean total fluence in ETI broadcasts also increases proportionally. Collective bounds apply to arbitrarily faint broadcasts as long as they are numerous enough, even when they fail to limit rare but very bright single broadcasts. The collective approach therefore has the distinct capability of setting constraints on pervasive Kardashev Type I–II transmitters in very distant galaxies, far below the detection threshold of typical SETI surveys.

The other advantage of this seemingly trivial limit is that it is unaffected by confusion. In fact, it demands we are near or in the confusion limit for the observation we are applying – the longer, wideband observations used to evaluate
\widehat{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu}}
reach this limit much sooner than typical SETI measurements. This mostly closes the gap in parameter space opened by the confusion limit in individualist approaches (section
VIII
).

VIII
Constraints on artificial radio transmissions in nearby galaxies

A comparison of the collective bound with individualist constraints using extant or planned SETI observations illustrates their relative merits, with the latter becoming weaker at greater distance. I present these comparisons for narrowband line searches in L-band, near 1.4 GHz. This region of the spectrum has historically been favored in radio SETI: background noise from synchrotron emission is quiet in this regime, and it contains the “water hole”, a band between radio lines presumed to be well-known to alien astrophysicists wanting to make contact
[
28
,
104
]
. The need to know which frequency to “meet at” was important when back ends covered at best only a few megahertz at a time, but modern instrumentation largely sidesteps the problem. Although there have been narrowband radio SETI surveys at much lower and higher frequencies, L-band is used here because it has the most results to compare with
[
133
,
44
, see]
, including extragalactic results
[
67
,
118
,
52
]
. But it is also commonly used to study galaxies’ radio continuum emission (e.g.,
Yun et al. 149
among many others). Thus, it is a natural choice for collective bounds derived from the literature. The basic ideas should apply for other types of broadcasts, however.

The broadcasts are assumed to all have the same luminosity
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
and an instantaneous abundance per unit frequency
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
. The power of individualist searches to detect lines is estimated using the chord model, assuming a single line (
{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}=1}
; equation
65
) and no dedrifting (
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
). The diffuse approximation is also applied. Sample confusion is evaluated according to equations
67
and
70
; noise confusion is also considered (equation
69
) as a robust limit against background broadcasts.

The range of mean broadcast luminosities considers spans many orders of magnitude. As a rough guide, I compare the results for different Kardashev scale levels. As in
Kardashev [73]
, the levels measure the
broadcast
effective isotropic power, with Type I representing a “planetary”-scale beacon (
\sim 10^{-10}\ {L}_{\odot}
), Type II a “stellar”-scale beacon (
\sim 1\ {L}_{\odot}
), and Type III a “galactic”-scale beacon (
\sim 10^{10}\ {L}_{\odot}
). When considered quantitatively, I adopt a Sagan-like normalization of
1\ {L}_{\odot}
for Type II, with a ratio of
10^{10}
between each class
[
25
, see]
.

VIII.1
Milky Way

The great majority of targeted SETI programs have observed things within our own Galaxy, focusing on nearby stars. At distances of parsecs instead of megaparsecs, we are able to detect far weaker transmissions – comparable to our planetary radars, although still well above any permanent broadcast we maintain
[
44
]
. There is, however, a great diversity of surveys, ranging from observations of one or two very nearby stars to large-scale surveys of millions of stars in the Galactic Center (Table
3
).

The regions of
1.4\ \textrm{GHz}
luminosity–abundance parameter space constrained by SETI literature are sketched as the colored shaded regions in Figure
4
. The bounds of these regions should be understood as order-of-magnitude estimates, as they assume a single characteristic distance for all stars in the sample and only include targeted stars. Broadcasts from background stars are detectable if bright enough
[
143
]
, increasing the mean number of sampled broadcasts at larger distances. Thus, the lower boundaries of the shaded regions excluded by individualist programs should actually bow downward at high
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
.

Unlike when we observe a single, distant metasociety, background societies within the Milky Way can have a wide range of distances. This spread in distances widens the fluence distribution to
\uppsi\left[\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}\propto\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{-5/2}
, broad enough to prevent confusion (Section
IV.2
). Confusion thus only sets in if the broadcasts from
each
society are confused, regardless of whether we consider the targeted stars or the background stars. Seeing as each society is located at a single location, one star, I evaluate confusion as if
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle=1
in equations
67
and
69
, representing the host of this nearest society. Confusion also depends on the drift rate spread. It is most severe when all lines have zero drift (solid regions); the maximum abundances allowed, when only noise confusion is an issue, are
\sim 10^{2}\text{--}10^{4.5}
times higher (hatched regions).

Also shown is a collective bound that would arise if the Milky Way lies on the far-infrared–radio correlation (FRC; see section
IX.1
) for star-forming galaxies (SFGs). I employ a bandwidth of
64\ \textrm{MHz}
, which is the bandwidth covered by CHIPASS, a recent 1.4 GHz southern sky survey used to estimate the Milky Way’s radio emission (
Calabretta et al. 18
; see also
Zheng et al. 151
). We cannot directly measure the radio luminosity of the Milky Way since we live inside it, but the assumption is validated by models of the Galaxy’s synchrotron emission
[
126
]
. The collective bound becomes more stringent for brighter broadcasts, of course, because fewer are needed to outshine the amount of radio emission that we actually expect from the Milky Way. Nonetheless, these models are fit to observations of the Milky Way that only cover parts of the radio spectrum, leaving open the possibility that some radio transmitters could be hiding in the “gaps”
[
151
, consider the literature data in Table 1 of ]
. The fact that we need to sample at least one broadcast to detect any artificial emission sets a
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
threshold, below which the collective bounds fail to be constraining.
14
14

14

Values of
\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{s}}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=d^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle/[d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\ln\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}]
much lower than one per Galaxy are meaningful because broadcasts turn on and off over time. The Galaxy could have
10^{-3}
active transmitters on average, for example, if only one turns on per millennium and transmits for just a year.
Future work can set collective bounds along individual sight lines by comparing the sky density of stars with the Galactic radio background.

Figure 4:
Order-of-magnitude constraints on ETI radio line broadcast luminosity and abundance in the Milky Way, assuming all broadcasts have the same luminosity. The collective bound from the assumption that the Galaxy lies on the FRC (grey) is shown against approximate individualist results from recent SETI surveys: R12 (red), P20 (green), H16 (violet), and G21 (gold). For the SETI surveys, solid shading is used for the constraints when all drift rates are zero; if only noise confusion is present, the hatched regions are constrained as well. These constraints assume that
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
is longer than the observational programs. The approximate EIRPs for the Arecibo planetary radar and Kardashev Type I (K1) and II (K2) broadcasts are marked.

It is clear from Figure
4
that the conventional individualist search strategy is highly effective in the Milky Way. Collective bounds set some weak limits on very low luminosity broadcasts (
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}\lesssim 10^{10}\ \textrm{W}
), and rule out the possibility that extreme confusion is preventing detection of bright broadcasts, but the
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
values involved are far-fetched: more than one broadcast per star per kilohertz for
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
up to
10^{13}\ \textrm{W}
, the EIRP of our brightest transmitters, the planetary radars on facilities like Arecibo. Kardashev Type I radio transmitters are limited to about 200 per star per gigahertz through the collective bound, compared with the individualist limits of
\sim 10^{-3}
per star per gigahertz from
Harp et al. [58]
. As we progress to higher broadcast EIRPs, surveys probing more distant regions of the Galaxy, like
Gajjar et al. [49]
, continue to be more restrictive than the collective bound because they cannot find even one to have a null result. For Kardashev Type II radio transmitters, the collective bound sets a limit of one per
50
million stars per gigahertz, while
Gajjar et al. [49]
set a limit about one order of magnitude stronger.

Surprisingly, though, the collective bound is the tightest of those in Figure
4
for the rarest, very bright transmitters – reaching about one per five billion stars per gigahertz. The plotted individualist SETI programs are targeted, covering only a small fraction of the Galaxy’s stars. There are also historical all-sky SETI surveys, and even continuum and HI radio surveys that could be brought to bear. The problem is that these all have limited frequency ranges: if there is only one transmitter in the Galaxy somewhere in the frequency band
0\text{--}10\ \textrm{GHz}
, it almost certainly falls outside the
0.4\text{--}0.5\ \textrm{MHz}
covered by Big Ear or META
[
39
,
67
]
, or the
40\text{--}60
MHz bandwidth of NVSS, HIPASS, or CHIPASS
[
31
,
97
,
18
]
. Breakthrough Listen is surveying the bulk of the Galactic Plane over hundreds of megahertz
[
69
]
, partly closing this window.

VIII.2
M31

M31 is the nearest large spiral galaxy to our own, the only one besides the Milky Way and the lower-mass M33 with a completed SETI survey since the turn of the century until very recently (Table
4
).
Gray & Mooley [52]
carried out a search for narrowband radio transmitters on the Jansky Very Large Array (JVLA). The Five-hundred-meter Aperture Spherical Radio Telescope (FAST) also will observe M31 in partnership with Breakthrough Listen
[
89
]
. Null results from these surveys are plotted with the collective bound from single-dish Effelsberg observations of total 1.4 GHz radio emission (
Beck et al. 13
; Table
5
) in Figure
5
.
