Note. — Abbreviations: LSR –local standard of rest; HSR – high spectral resolution.

Gray & Mooley [52]
and future FAST limits especially can already rule out a wide chunk of
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
–
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
parameter space, but they are much more limited than Milky Way observations. At the bright end, the JVLA line search and the Effelsberg-derived collective bound only probe
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\gtrsim 100\text{--}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000\ \textrm{GHz}^{-1}\mathrm{M31}^{-1}
because they have limited bandwidth, the same problem encountered with the Milky Way. Because M31 covers a smaller region of the sky, it is a relatively simple matter for FAST to scan all of M31 with its wide bandwidth and close this window. The collective bound is the only constraint on line broadcasts with
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}\lesssim 10^{19}\ \textrm{W}
. Since M31 is much fainter than the Milky Way is presumed to be in L-band despite having more stars, the collective bound is about
10
times stronger than for our Galaxy. With the collective bound, we can rule out the existence of hundreds of Kardashev Type I transmitters per gigahertz around each star in M31, for example, a population easily built by an advanced galactic metasociety. Additionally, confusion sets in much sooner for M31 than in the Milky Way, when there is
\sim 0.01\text{--}10
line broadcast per star per gigahertz.

Figure 5:
Current and forthcoming constraints on radio line broadcasts in M31 (left) and three example Virgo cluster ellipticals (right), assuming that all broadcasts in each galaxy have the same EIRP
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
. The G17 observations of M31 exclude the red shaded region, while example future observations with FAST and GBT are in blue and green, respectively. Solid shading applies to individualist constraints when
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}=0
, and hatched shading is for the noise confusion limit at high
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}
(vertical for M49, diagonal for M31 and M87, horizontal for M59).

VIII.3
Virgo cluster ellipticals

Of the galaxies in the Breakthrough Listen nearby galaxies survey
[
69
]
, the early-type Virgo Cluster galaxies are among the most massive and the most distant, which favor collective bounds. On the other hand, they also include radio galaxies, which are radio-bright, and thus their collective bounds are weakened, allowing a more luminous broadcast population. Three of these galaxies are chosen here as representatives: M87, one of the brightest radio sources on the sky; M49, the largest and a much quieter radio galaxy; and M59, an elliptical with no detected 1.4 GHz radio emission at all (Table
5
). All three have been observed with the Green Bank Telescope (GBT) during Breakthrough Listen’s survey (
Isaacson et al. 69
; Table
4
). The L-band beam width is much larger than the effective radii of these galaxies, so a single pointing will cover almost all of the stellar population.
15
15

15

The effective radii are comparable to the beam width of FAST at 1.4 GHz, or GBT at
\sim 10\ \textrm{GHz}
, however.

The line broadcast constraints in Figure
5
show how collective bounds become progressively stronger as the galaxy’s observed radio flux goes down. In fact, collective bounds already rule out a substantial chunk of M59’s EIRP-abundance parameter space being probed by the GBT. This follows from how faint it is in the radio, less than
\sim 40\ {L}_{\odot}\,\textrm{GHz}^{-1}
. GBT’s advantages in M59 are for a “corner” of rare, sub-Kardashev II transmitters (
10^{24}\text{--}10^{27}\ \textrm{W}
), and ultrarare bright transmitters, where the need to detect at least one broadcast limits the collective bound because of the relatively narrow bandwidth of VLSS (
42\ \textrm{MHz}
;
Condon et al. 31
). Even the advantage for ultrarare lines could be eliminated by measuring M59’s diffuse emission across a few gigahertz of bandwidth. Of course, the issue is not just that the collective bounds are strong for the radio-silent M59, but that the individualist bounds are weak because of the great distances and insensitivity to faint broadcasts. An isotropic narrowband transmitter in the Virgo Cluster would have to be about as luminous as Proxima Centauri to be detected by GBT.

Individualist bounds are more important for M49 and especially M87, since more abundant and/or brighter transmitters would be needed to match their radio luminosities. In all three galaxies, however, confusion sets in no later than when there is about one line broadcast per thousand stars per gigahertz, and one per million stars per gigahertz for lines with zero drift. Thus, confusion is a legitimate concern, as these abundances are plausible in a fully populated galaxy.

IX
General expectations for the collective bound

IX.1
The Far-infrared–Radio correlation and collective bounds

With the exception of some active galactic nuclei, galaxies are radio quiet. This makes the radio band well suited to collective bounds, because it would not take much power for ETIs to overwhelm the natural radio emission of a galaxy. Low radio background in fact motivated radio transmitters as possible technosignatures
[
28
]
. In the range below
\sim 10\ \textrm{GHz}
, the radio emission of most galaxies, if present, is dominated by synchrotron emission from cosmic-ray electrons and positrons, with
\mathfrak{F}_{\nu;\mathrm{g}}\propto\nu^{-\alpha}
where
\alpha\sim 0.7\text{--}0.8
for most normal SFGs
[
30
]
. These are accelerated by phenomena associated with young massive stars and thus trace star-formation rate. In fact, most SFGs lie on the FRC, with a ratio of far-infrared to 1.4 GHz radio luminosity ratio that is constant within a factor of about two at
{z}\sim 0

[
149
]
. For a Kroupa initial mass function,
Murphy et al. [100]
relate the mass star formation rate
\dot{\mathnormal{M}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{g}}
and radio brightness:

\left(\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathrm{g}}(1.4\ \textrm{GHz})}{\textrm{W}\,\textrm{Hz}^{-1}}\right)={1.57\times 10^{-21}}\left(\frac{\dot{\mathnormal{M}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{g}}}{{M}_{\odot}\,\textrm{yr}^{-1}}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(87)

although dwarf galaxies have previously been found to be radio-quieter
[
14
]
. SFGs also become somewhat brighter in radio synchrotron emission at high redshift
[
38
]
.

Additionally, the majority of SFGs at a given redshift and SFR have similar stellar masses
{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}
, a relation sometimes called the “main sequence” of SFGs.
Speagle et al. [122]
find a best-fit relation that can be expressed as

\left(\frac{\dot{\mathnormal{M}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{g}}}{{M}_{\odot}\,\textrm{yr}^{-1}}\right)=10^{{-0.13}}\left(\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{{10^{10}}\ {M}_{\odot}}\right)^{0.49}\mathchar 59\relax

(88)

at
{z}=0
, in terms of the Kroupa initial mass function. The main sequence and FRC can be combined to estimate the radio emission of most SFGs at
{z}=0
:

\left(\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathrm{g}}(1.4\ \textrm{GHz})}{\textrm{W}\,\textrm{Hz}^{-1}}\right)=1.17\times 10^{21}\left(\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{10^{10}{M}_{\odot}}\right)^{0.49}.

(89)

In turn, this radio luminosity can be plugged into equations
80
,
85
, and
86
to yield a collective bound. The population of artificial narrowband radio transmitters in nonactive
{z}=0
galaxies is limited by the FRC to:

{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\nu)}\lesssim 0.23\ \textrm{GHz}^{-1}\,\textrm{star}^{-1}\ \left(\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{10^{10}{M}_{\odot}}\right)^{0.49}\\
{\cdot}\left(\frac{{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{10^{20}\ \textrm{W}}\right)\left(\frac{\nu}{1.4\ \textrm{GHz}}\right)^{-\alpha}\left(\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{m}_{\star}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{g}}}{0.2\ {M}_{\odot}}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(90)

within a factor of a few, as long as the broadcasts are individually much fainter than
\sim\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathrm{g}}(1.4\ \textrm{GHz})
. For comparison, present SETI surveys of nearby galaxies are expected to be sensitive to broadcasts with
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim 10^{19}\text{--}10^{20}\ \textrm{W}

[
69
]
. The FRC automatically implies that, in the vast majority of SFGs, most stars do not have a transmitter that bright in the examined frequency ranges.

A large fraction of stellar mass at low redshift is found in red early-type galaxies
[
99
]
. Although these galaxies can contain residual star formation and its attendant radio emission, by and large, most of them are radio quiet
[
103
]
. Thus, collective bounds on radio emission can be even more powerful than for SFGs (section
VIII.3
). Large early-type galaxies typically do have radio emission, however
[
112
]
.

IX.2
Prospects for the collective bound for broadcasts at higher energies and other messengers

The collective bound only lets us say that the ETI broadcast population is fainter than the galaxy as a whole. It thus favors wavebands where the galaxy’s luminosity is known to be a small fraction of the bolometric luminosity.

Infrared

SFGs are bright in infrared because of the presence of thermal dust emission, with some residual contribution of stellar emission at short wavelengths
[
120
, e.g.,]
. Waste heat technosignature searches effectively use the collective bound
[
54
,
50
]
, and they have focused on the mid-infrared because the classical Dyson sphere is “habitable”
[
42
]
. SFGs are fortuitously fainter in mid-infrared, but nonetheless about
\sim 10\text{--}20\%
of the emission is released at
5\text{--}20\ \mu\textrm{m}
(though in the form of narrow bands from small dust grains; e.g.,
Dale et al. 36
), limiting these types of searches
[
145
]
. Quiescent galaxies, however, including most ellipticals and lenticulars, are deficient in dust and infrared emission, though not entirely bereft of it
[
135
,
121
]
, allowing more stringent collective bounds
[
145
]
.

Optical

Although optical transmissions are a long-sought technosignature, collective bounds are of dubious value for constraining them. Starlight is peaked in near-infrared, optical, and ultraviolet, so in order for broadcasts to contribute meaningfully to a galaxy’s optical luminosity, they must practically outshine all the stars in the galaxy! Optical SETI’s technosignatures are viable because we are not looking at the mean emission of a target galaxy, but for individualist rare fluctuations – ultranarrowband lines or ultrashort pulses that stick out from the normal emission
[
115
,
68
,
5
]
. Optical megastructure searches instead seek abnormal extinction of the starlight by the putative structures
[
9
,
150
]
.

Conceivably, the collective emission could be detectable if it was beamed, probably resulting in a smooth spectral component not found in nearby galaxies. Alternatively, broadcasts clustering around a “magic frequency” aggregate into an unnatural emission line in the galaxy’s spectrum, which may stand out as being at an unusual wavelength or by apparently dominating the galaxy’s luminosity if beamed without the usual signs of intense star formation or an active nucleus. Finally, the discreteness of extremely bright optical transmitters with
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg 1\ {L}_{\odot}
might be detectable from galactic surface brightness fluctuations.

High energy radiation

Collective bounds should be moderately useful for constraining broadcasts of high-energy radiation. X-ray SETI has been considered theoretically a few times in the literature, although observational results are scant
[
33
,
65
,
82
]
. The same is true for neutrino SETI
[
128
,
84
,
83
,
81
]
, and gamma-ray SETI is even more underdeveloped (for a rare exception, see
Harris 59
,
Harris 60
). The collective bound serves as a default upper limit.

Most galaxies are X-ray emitters, as low-mass X-ray binaries alone contribute a minimum luminosity tracing stellar mass, about
\sim 10^{-4.5}\ {L}_{\odot}{/}{M}_{\odot}
at
z=0
and roughly
10
times higher at
z\sim 2\text{--}3

[
86
,
88
]
. Star-forming galaxies also have soft X-ray emission from their interstellar medium and hard X-rays from high-mass X-ray binaries tracing star-formation rate
[
86
,
98
]
. X-ray telescopes are powerful enough to detect the comparatively low X-ray luminosities of normal galaxies out to cosmological distances, so the collective bound can be applied for a large number of galaxies
[
87
]
. Although not as stringent as the radio collective bounds, X-ray collective bounds are likely to be useful, especially given how underdeveloped high-energy SETI is.

In principle, gamma-ray and neutrino emission from galaxies is faint enough that the collective bound should be very useful. Observations of nearby SFGs indicate that typically
\sim 10^{-5}
to
10^{-4}
of the bolometric power is released as GeV gamma rays
[
6
]
. Quiescent galaxies may be even fainter in gamma rays
[
91
, for theoretical discussion, see]
. Our sensitivity to gamma rays is comparatively weak, however: about a dozen star-forming galaxies beyond the Milky Way have been detected, only four of them (the Magellanic Clouds, M31, and M33) not intense starburst galaxies
[
4
,
8
]
. We thus cannot rule out that many galaxies are abnormally gamma-ray bright from broadcasts.
Abdollahi et al. [4]
report a current flux detection limit of
\sim 2\times 10^{-12}\ \textrm{erg}\,\textrm{cm}^{-2}\,\textrm{s}^{-1}
from
Fermi-LAT
for GeV gamma-ray sources with an
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}\propto\nu^{-1}
spectrum. The collective bound applied to narrowband sources suggests a maximum abundance of

\nu\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\sim 6\times 10^{-5}\ \textrm{star}^{-1}\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{1\ {L}_{\odot}}\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{10^{11}}\left(\frac{{d}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}}{10\ \textrm{Mpc}}\right)^{2}\right]^{-1}.

(91)

Neutrino limits are also quite poor. IceCube reports the ability to detect point sources in the northern TeV-PeV neutrino sky with fluxes below
\sim 3\times 10^{-12}\ \textrm{erg}\,\textrm{cm}^{-2}\,\textrm{s}^{-1}
, assuming 1:1:1 flavor ratios and an
E^{-2}
spectrum
[
1
]
. The resulting limit is similar:

\nu\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\sim 9\times 10^{-5}\ \textrm{star}^{-1}\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{1\ {L}_{\odot}}\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{10^{11}}\left(\frac{{d}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}}{10\ \textrm{Mpc}}\right)^{2}\right]^{-1}.

(92)

Still, given that we have essentially no gamma-ray or neutrino SETI constraints, even weak collective bounds from upper limits can serve as a starting point. These limits are already sufficient to eliminate the possibility that most big nearby galaxies like M81 are home to ETIs that convert
\gtrsim 0.01\%
of available starlight into GeV gamma rays or TeV neutrinos.

Gravitational waves

Gravitational waves have occasionally been suggested as a messenger for ETI broadcasts
[
63
]
. The difficulty of gravitational-wave SETI is the sheer weakness of gravity. LIGO and other gravitational wave observatories can detect black hole mergers from hundreds of megaparsecs away simply because they are the most powerful known events in the Universe
[
2
]
. Still, LIGO and Virgo have been able to set limits on point sources of gravitational waves with flat spectra at around 25 Hz. The most recent limits are of order
10^{-10}
to
10^{-9}\ \textrm{erg}\,\textrm{cm}^{-2}\,\textrm{s}^{{-1}}\,\textrm{Hz}^{-1}
, depending on location on the sky
[
3
]
. For a population of persistent narrowband transmitters, the collective bound gives an abundance limit of

\nu\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\lesssim 0.1\text{--}0.9\ \textrm{star}^{-1}\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{1\ {L}_{\odot}}\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{10^{11}}\left(\frac{{d}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}}{10\ \textrm{Mpc}}\right)^{2}\right]^{-1}.

(93)

We can therefore reject the existence of Kardashev Type III metasocieties broadcasting all of their power in
\sim 25\ \textrm{Hz}
gravitational waves in the nearest large galaxies. This still leaves the rest of the gravitational-wave spectrum unexplored, however, and allows the existence of less powerful broadcasts of gravitational waves even in the nearest galaxies.

X
Conclusion

I consider two general types of constraints derived from the collection of measurements we make in a SETI survey, the individualist signal-to-noise constraint and the collective bound. Both types of limits are subject to a discreteness criterion: they cannot constrain models where broadcasts, or the transmitting societies, are sufficiently rare.

The individualist approach searches for single anomalous measurements that are incompatible with a natural background, a spike rising above the background past some signal-to-noise threshold. Most SETI results employ the individualist strategy. Only a single, sufficiently bright broadcast in the sample is necessary for a detection, which makes it useful if ETIs are rare. But if there are too many broadcasts per observation, with too narrow a fluence distribution, the signal-to-noise ratio falls because they start to overlap, ultimately resulting in the confusion limit where no detection is possible. The sample variance sets an upper limit of

\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim\left(\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\right)/\sqrt{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}

to the signal-to-noise ratio when the broadcasts are all about equally bright. Sample confusion occurs when this ratio falls below a threshold
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}
for detection; in some cases, the noise of the mutually interfering broadcasts can produce a noise confusion effect. I present calculations about this degradation, including estimates for when confusion sets in for continuum sources,

\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\right]^{-1}

and narrowband radio lines,

\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}(1+\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}})\right]^{-1}

{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]^{-1}}

under standard assumptions (Table
2
) with broadcasts of equal brightness. Confusion only happens for dense populations – about one per channel per resolution element for narrowband observations – but
a priori
we cannot rule those out. As near as the Virgo Cluster, billions of stars and all their broadcasts can be blended into one radio beam, preventing detection if
\sim 1
in
1000
stars has a gigahertz radio “beacon.” As we approach the confusion limit, more abundant broadcasts actually
worsen
the prospects for detection.

The collective bound,

{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\lesssim{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}+\frac{C^{\prime 2}}{2}\left(1+\sqrt{1+\frac{4}{C^{\prime 2}}{\hat{\mathnormal{N}}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

follows from the aggregate emission, and is the simple observation that ETI broadcasts cannot outshine the galaxy’s observed emission itself. It is unable to make a detection but is robust to confusion and uncertainty about the form of the broadcasts. Generally speaking, the collective bound is more suitable for distant galaxies, because it only needs a measurement of the luminosity and because confusion is more severe at larger distances. The collective bound closes the window opened by confusion: we are not in fact missing vast populations of ETIs because their broadcasts are overlapping with each other. Collective bounds are most useful in wavebands where galaxies are faint and our instruments are sensitive, especially radio but also possibly X-rays. Radio collective bounds of similar strength apply to all galaxies on the FRC, out to cosmological distances, and are even more constraining for radio-quiet quiescent galaxies. They set a limit of a few hundred Kardashev Type I radio transmitters per gigahertz in galaxies like the Milky Way and about a few hundred thousand Kardashev Type II GeV gamma-ray or TeV neutrino transmitters in nearby galaxies, and they constrain Local Group Kardashev Type III gravitational wave transmitters in the band observed by LIGO.

Only the simplest properties of the aggregate emission of ETI populations have been considered. More powerful constraints could be found by exploiting the detailed statistical properties of this emission using the broadcast distributions. The underlying discrete nature of broadcasts affects the flux statistics
[
34
]
, with an implied presence of Poisson fluctuations and spatial and temporal correlations. An example of this kind of approach can be found in the
P(D)
method of constraining faint cosmic radio sources by examining fluctuations of the radio background
[
113
,
29
,
32
,
138
]
and extended to X-rays and gamma-rays
[
114
,
93
]
. Likewise, measuring stellar Poisson fluctuations serves as a means of probing the stellar content and distance of other galaxies
[
136
,
109
, as in]
. Statistical methods allow us to glimpse deeper into the ETI luminosity function than we can hope with near-future targeted searches, sweeping another layer off the “cosmic haystack”
[
146
, c.f.,]
.

As with Paper I, I thank the referee, whose dedicated efforts resulted in detailed and helpful comments on this paper. I also thank the Breakthrough Listen program for their support. Funding for
Breakthrough Listen
research is sponsored by the Breakthrough Prize Foundation (
https://breakthroughprize.org/
). In addition, I acknowledge the use of NASA’s Astrophysics Data System and arXiv for this research.

Appendix A
Derivations for the mean and sample variance of the aggregate emission

As before, we assume that all the broadcasts come from a single metasociety in a single galaxy (possibly the Milky Way). Suppose the mean aggregate emission is the sum of the mean individual signals.
16
16

16

T
The total energy collected in radio observations is not generally the sum of the energies of each broadcast and the background, because they interfere with each other. But it can be shown that the mean energies do add linearly, so the condition still applies.
Suppose the collected emission adds linearly. The sample-conditionalized mean tells us the mean emission that is collected during an observation from a fixed collection of societies and broadcasts:

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle={\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}})\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}.

(A1)

In order to calculate the mean over all samples, we start by calculating the emission from a single society. Because a society is localized, all the broadcasts have the same dilution (distance). If the instrumental response has no dependence on frequency or time within the window
\mathsf{o}
(equation
28
), all the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}
terms will be the same as well, depending only on the sky position of the society
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{C}}
. Now, this is not precisely true: actual observations do not have perfectly sharp bandpasses, and beam size falls with frequency, for example, but this assumption should still suffice for typical SETI observations, which involve fine channels. Then, given the society’s parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}
, we have

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{C}}){\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{C}}){y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}{\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(A2)

where the second equality follows from Campbell’s formula.

Now this quantity can be interpreted as a random variable describing the society. We can therefore calculate the total mean with another application of Campbell’s formula:

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{C}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}.

(A3)

Plugging in the societal distribution from equation
23
, we get

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\mathcal{A}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\int_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}){y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}){\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{d{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}}d{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}.

(A4)

This assumes that
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}=\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
,
\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}=\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
.

The sample variance is slightly trickier, because of the double sum over the broadcast and societal samples. To start, we note that the societies are a Poisson point process for a metasociety of given
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}
, and so Campbell’s second formula applies:

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\mathbb{V}\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\left\langle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle\right]=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{C}}}{\left\langle\Biggl(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\Biggr)^{2}\Bigg|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle}{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}

(A5)

While the background has variance, the mean background is a constant and does not contribute to sample variance (the background is always “sampled”). By the definition of variance,

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{C}}}\left[\mathbb{V}\left[{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]+\left\langle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}.

(A6)

For the variance term in the integral, we again note that broadcasts are a Poisson point process for a society with given
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}
, and so

\mathbb{V}\left[\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\left\langle\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{C}})^{2}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{C}}^{2}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}{\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}}.

(A7)

A difficulty arises if the transmittance is not a simple function of position: the first term in equation
A6
uses
\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}
, while the second uses
\left\langle{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}
, and unlike the amount of emission, it is not independent of position. If the transmittance is solely a function of position, and not time or frequency, then it has a single value for each society and the two quantities are equal. This, of course, cannot be entirely accurate, but if we are dealing with observations of moderate bandwidths in the absence of absorption lines, we can approximate the extinction as constant over that bandwidth, and
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}={\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}
. Again assuming that
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}=\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
,

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]=\mathcal{A}^{2}{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left[\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right]\int_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{o}}}[\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}){y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}){{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star})}]^{2}\frac{d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}.

(A8)

We can define the variable

{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{[}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}{(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}})}{{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}{]}^{n}{\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(A9)

for all
n
, in which case our assumptions give us

\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle=\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\int_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{x}}}[\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}){y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}){{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star})}]^{n}\frac{d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}{\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(A10)

Equation
A4
reduces to

\left\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\mathcal{A}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\mathchar 59\relax

(A11)

(A12)

When the broadcasts are all part of a single distant galaxy and extinction is negligible or constant (e.g., from a smooth foreground screen like Earth’s atmosphere), the dilution factor of each broadcast can be assumed to be the same, allowing the substitution

\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}^{n}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle.

(A13)

\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}[\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{M};\mathsf{x}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}})]^{n}\mathchar 59\relax

(A14)

(A15)

Appendix B
Further details on calculation of radio energy statistics

B.1
Noise variance in radio detection

This appendix provides a summary of the mean noise variance calculation for radio broadcasts,
\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]\right\rangle
. We start by describing the raw amplitudes with a complex phasor,
\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}=|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}|\exp(i\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}})
(with
i=\sqrt{-1}
). As in section
V.1
, I adopt a convention that
\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}=|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}|^{2}
. The
\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}
may be fully randomized for incoherent signals, be constant for coherent signals, or display correlations for partially coherent signals. The measured energy can be regarded as the sum of an unvarying mean component that is a measure of the mean energy output of the broadcast and a fluctuation component with zero mean (section
V.1
):

\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}={\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle+\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}=}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}})\mathfrak{h}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}+{\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}){y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}+{\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}}.

(B1)

Likewise, the phases may be linked by a relation, but individually they have a uniform distribution over
[-\pi\mathchar 59\relax\pi)
and I generally consider them independent between broadcasts and modes. These are summed together with a background of noise with
\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}=|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}|\exp(i\varphi_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}})
. The background amplitude has a complex Gaussian distribution, with
\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle=\left\langle|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}\right\rangle=k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Furthermore, the background energy has an exponential (
\chi_{2}^{2}
) distribution, with
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}\right]=\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle^{2}
. Thus, the measured amplitude for mode
\upmu
is

\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}=|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}|\exp(i\varphi_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}|\exp(i\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}){=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}|\exp(i\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}})}.

(B2)

The adjoined sample
\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{j}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}=\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{j}}^{\mathrm{B}}\cup\{{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathfrak{n}}}\}
includes the background noise with amplitude
\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathfrak{n}}
, which is, for the purposes of these calculations, a special broadcast that is always present.

Square-law detectors measure the total energy as
\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}=\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}{\mathfrak{A}_{\upmu}}^{\ast}
:

\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\upmu}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}}|\cos(\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}-\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}}).

(B3)

Then, the energy from several modes can be added together to yield the energy measured by the observation. Let
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}
:

\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}+\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}}|\cos(\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}-\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}}).

(B4)

The broadcasts in a sample have independent phases, giving us
\left\langle\cos(\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}-\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}})|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=0
if
\mathrm{B}_{1}\neq\mathrm{B}_{2}
. Since
\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}
,
\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle={\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\mathsf{o}}({\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}}){{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}
, and the broadcasts have independent phases,

\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}){{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B5)

just as in equation
53
. It can then be shown that

\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\mathchar 59\relax

(B6)

when transmittance depends only on position, just as for additive emission (equation
A11
).

For the noise variance, we must calculate
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\left\langle{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}}^{2}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle^{2}
. To start,

{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}}^{2}=\left(\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right)^{2}+2\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}_{1}}\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}|\cos(\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}-\varphi_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}})\\
+\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}||\mathfrak{a}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}|\cos(\varphi_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}-\varphi_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}})\cos(\varphi_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}}-\varphi_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}}).

(B7)

Note that the final term includes in the summation the cases when
\upmu_{1}=\upmu_{2}
. After averaging, the middle term vanishes. The final sum has nonzero terms when
\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}=\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}
and
\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}=\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}
, or when
\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}=\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}
and
\mathrm{B}_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}=\mathrm{B}_{2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}
, generally when
\upmu_{1}=\upmu_{2}
. The other terms are taken to average to zero, under the assumption that the phases of the broadcasts are independent. If two different pairs of broadcasts are chosen for
\upmu_{1}
and
\upmu_{2}
, then the phase differences between each pair should also be independent. If the same pair of broadcasts is chosen for
\upmu_{1}
and
\upmu_{2}
, conceivably
\cos(\varphi_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{1}}-\varphi_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}_{2}})=\cos(\varphi_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{1}}-\varphi_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}_{2}})
if
\mathrm{B}_{1}
and
\mathrm{B}_{2}
are coherent and always at the same exact frequency, but otherwise the phase differences should be scrambled by different frequencies and drift rates, so those cases are assumed to average to zero too.

After proceeding through the algebra, we find

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}\\
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{1}}\neq\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}_{2}}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}_{2}}\right\rangle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\mathnormal{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}\ddagger}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]{.}

(B8)

The covariance term contains all the information about the coherence of the signal. It is given by

\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]=\left\langle\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\Updelta\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle.

(B9)

This property of the fluctuations is described by the
g^{(2)}_{\mathrm{B}}
function (Appendix
B.3
).

The noise variance is the average of this quantity over all possible samples. Under the assumption that the properties of the broadcast population and noise do not vary with mode,

\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]\right\rangle=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right]+\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathrm{Cov}[{\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}}]\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}

(B10)

for extinction that depends only on position. The average over covariances does
not
include the background noise (i.e., the sample is not adjoined), which has already been accounted for under the assumption that it is incoherent.

B.2
Incoherent radio broadcasts

Incoherent sources have complex Gaussian amplitudes, and their sum is also a complex Gaussian. Thus, we generally expect the energy to be exponentially distributed, with
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle^{2}
. Furthermore, the noise should be independent between modes, so that
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\upmu}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]
. These conclusions are borne out from equation
B8
, if we adopt
17
17

17

Strictly speaking,
{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(|\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{2}}|)-1
is not exactly zero for nonzero time delays – its form is related to the Fourier transform of the (channel) bandpass-filtered signal
[
131
]
. Polyphase filterbanks use samples from several coarsely channelized spectra separated in time to suppress sidebands
[
106
]
, so we do expect nonzero covariance. However, the covariance should be small, decreasing rapidly as the delay grows past
\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}
.

{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]}=\begin{cases}\left\langle\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle^{2}&\text{if}~\upmu=\upmu_{1}=\upmu_{2}\\
0&\text{if}~\upmu=\upmu_{1}\neq\upmu_{2}\end{cases}.

(B11)

According to equation
B11
, the only nonzero covariance terms are the variance terms, so the noise variance is

\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]\right\rangle=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right)\right].

(B12)

When expanded, there are five terms in the noise variance: (1) a constant term from system noise and natural background emission; (2) a term linear in
\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
, describing interference between the noise and the broadcasts; (3) a
\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle^{2}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}
term describing the interference between the broadcasts among all societies; (4) a
\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{{2}}
term for the interference between different broadcasts from the same society; and (5) a
\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
term describing the wave noise from self-interference. The last term is the mean self-noise of an individual broadcast
[
79
, e.g.,]
, a noise that is intrinsic to the source even in the absence of all other noise. Terms three and four might also be included as “self-noise” of the broadcast population as a whole.

The noise variance depends on how each broadcast’s energy is apportioned into modes. If the energy is clumped into a small fraction of the modes summed in an observation, the noise variance of incoherent broadcasts is increased: the
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}
for occupied cells is
\propto({\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}})^{-2}
, while the number of occupied cells is only proportional to
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}
(Figure
6
).

Figure 6:
Radio observations (
\mathsf{o}_{1}
and
\mathsf{o}_{2}
) measure collected energy by summing over the power in a number of modes (amplitudes). A spectrogram on the left shows two broadcasts, one for each observation. The mean total energy intercepted from the shown broadcasts is the same in
\mathsf{o}_{1}
and
\mathsf{o}_{2}
, but although the
\mathsf{o}_{1}
broadcast’s energy is spread out among all cells (light shading), the
\mathsf{o}_{2}
broadcast’s energy is concentrated in one cell (dark shading) and the other cells only have background (white). On the right, the resulting probability densities for the energy per mode (top) and energy per observation (bottom) are shown. The noise variance of
{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}_{2}}}
is greater than that of
{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}_{2}}}
despite having the same mean, a result of the heavier PDF tail.

The full variance for incoherent broadcasts is

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]=\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right)\right]\\
+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right).

(B13)

For convenience and intuition’s sake when calculating the effective signal-to-noise ratio, we can express this in terms of
\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]
(equation
59
) to find the generalization of equation
57
:

\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{2}}=\left(1+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)\\
+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right).

(B14)

B.2.1
The box model and incoherent broadcasts

In the box model, the modes form a contiguous window in time and frequency, with
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}=|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
. The mean measured energy
\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right\rangle
can be found with equation
B6
.

The full expression for the variance is complicated even with the simplifying approximations of the box model. Narrowband emission in the form of lines is coherent and likely to have frequency drift; they are treated with the chord model (Appendix
B.3.3
). The box model itself is very useful for wideband broadcasts. If
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
, the broadcasts are necessarily incoherent.

I scale
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
and
{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle}
in equation
B14
under the assumption that the observation only includes one beam, covering the same sky field in all observations. Thus,
{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle/\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle
. The equations include means of the polarization factor
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\sum_{p\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p;\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}})
and its square, with
\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|/2
.

For incoherent pulses (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
,
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\upmu}
),

\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{2}}\approx\left(1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\frac{4\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}}\left(1+{\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}}\right)\right.\\
\left.+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\right)\right].

(B15)

Hisses (
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
,
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
) include steady continuum sources and have

\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{2}}\approx\left(1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\left[\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\frac{4\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}}\left(1+{\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\right)\right.\\
\left.+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\right)\right].

(B16)

Lines with
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
necessarily have a coherence time
{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}^{-1}\geq\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}
(see section
V.4
).
18
18

18

No signal can meaningfully have
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\upmu}
and
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\upmu}
simultaneously, so there is no strict blip regime for radio broadcasts.

B.3
Coherent radio broadcasts

The mean energy in coherent broadcasts is correlated between different times. This leads to a prolonged “plateau” in the covariance where it takes on values of order the variance. In a fully coherent broadcast, there is no variance and thus no covariance. A partially coherent broadcast can be viewed as band-limited Gaussian white noise, the sum of a continuum of small random sinusoids with slightly different frequencies, and so fluctuates with wave noise on long timescales.

B.3.1
Dedrifting, energy conservation, and the chord model

Coherent radio broadcasts are narrowband, immediately suggesting the use of the chord model. The details of how we look for putative signals with incoherent dedrifting complicates this relatively simple picture, however.

A line drifts across
|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
of bandwidth over an observation, a long-term smearing effect. If this is greater than
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}>1
, the center of the line moves into different frequency channels in different dynamic spectra. Summing along one channel results in the loss of power. The modes are first summed into intermediate data blocks (
\mathsf{d}
), each with the same bandwidth as the channel as a whole (
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{d}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
). Each data block
\mathsf{d}_{j}
is then shifted in frequency by an amount
-(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{d}_{j}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{o}})\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
, “straightening out” the skewed lines on the spectrogram that represent drifting signals. A broadcast that starts out at drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}

in the inertial frame
then is treated as though it has a new drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
. This simple translate-and-add method splits the window “box” into several boxes strung along a chord with
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
in the inertial-frame (raw) spectrogram. As such, the chord model does not actually apply after dedrifting – we have a “pseudochord” model for these observations, as opposed to the “true chord” model that applies when
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
.

The amount of time that a broadcast spends in the reconstructed “box” in the dedrifted frame can be shorter or longer than the naive prediction based on
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}
, a result of “shadowing” effects of the different inertial-frame boxes and the chord running out of the constructed observation window and then reentering it. The effects of the dedrifting can be described by a parameter

\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\tau};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{CHORD}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}\frac{1+|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B17)

where
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{CHORD}}
is the duration that would apply if we were working in a “true” chord model with
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
.

There are four basic regimes of behavior, as shown in Figure
7
. Regime A is closest to the simple chord model, where a chord is not likely to pass straight through the bandwidth of a channel during a single data window. Regime D is also fairly simple – in both the inertial frame and the dedrifted frame, the broadcast has high drift rate, passing through the observation window once and never returning. Regime C represents a relatively successful dedrifting of a high-
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
broadcast. A well-known issue with this dedrifting method is that the data block’s duration imposes a short-term smearing in frequency: the broadcast enters and leaves the datum’s bandwidth before the next datum can correct, spreading the energy over many channels. Like long-term smearing, this reduces sensitivity. Regime B, however, is not as familiar, because it represents a case where dedrifting
ruins
a broadcast already with low drift rate. In this case, the data block’s duration imposes a minimum “exposure” that is much longer than naively expected from
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}
– a kind of apparent short-term desmearing. However, it is also possible for a broadcast ostensibly in the frequency range of the dedrifted channel to be “missed” as the data blocks skip over wide intervals in frequency (pink shading in the figure). Although regime B does not apply to a detection, it still comes up when considering the sample variance of the broadcast population.

Figure 7:
The total energy from a narrowband broadcast sampled by a window, as well as the mean number of broadcasts sampled, depends on both the intrinsic drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
and the “observed” drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}
. Dedrifting slices up spectrograms into data bins of duration
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
, applying a frequency shift linear in time, inducing an apparent drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}
(dotted lines). Within each data bin, however, there is no frequency shift, so the broadcast behaves as if it has the original drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
(solid lines). Examples from each of the four regimes are shown. Within each regime, the observation window is constructed from data subwindows (blue boxes). The top spectrogram in each regime sketches the spectrogram before dedrifting is applied; the bottom one shows the spectrogram constructed by dedrifting. In regimes B, C, and D, the line is smeared (grey checkerboard shading). The pink hatched shading for the regime B spectrograms shows where a line is missed by the observation, falling through the gaps despite falling in the nominal bandwidth of the dedrifted channel. A line in one of these gaps would be picked up in one of the neighboring channels. The
\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\tau};\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
factor expresses the ratio between the time the chord spends in the dedrifted observation and the time predicted in the true chord model.

Both the mean number and typical time the broadcast spend in an observation are affected by the dedrifting process. The product of the two is constant, a fundamental result that is needed for energy conservation. Suppose we had a population of broadcasts, all with the same drift rate and the same luminosity. The mean amount of energy intercepted per window is proportional to
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\propto\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
. Now suppose that the instantaneous bandwidth of the observation is
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
at all times, even though which frequencies are covered can shift from one moment to the next. When averaged over all samples, the mean aggregate emission of a population is unaffected by drift rate, as long as the abundance of lines is approximately constant over the entire bandwidth spanned by the observation. This is a consequence of frequency translation symmetry and conservation of energy. If a larger spread of drift rate resulted in
\left\langle\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
going down for one channel, then it should do so for the next one, and the one after that, and the one after that, and so on. That would mean that the summed energy over the bandwidth
\gtrsim 2\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}
has also decreased. But where could the energy have gone? The broadcasts are still shining as brightly; they merely have shifted around within that bandwidth. Additionally, for every broadcast that leaves the window, we expect one broadcast to enter on average.

Thus, as a general principle,

\frac{d\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}.

(B18)

Integrating over drift rate also gives us

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}.

(B19)

Finally, putting the luminosity back in, a robust result of energy conservation is

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}.

(B20)

This average applies also to weighted numbers, like
