\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle
and even
\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle
:

\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle{Y}_{0;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}~\text{and}~\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{n}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B21)

which can be seen by applying the energy conservation principle to the population in each small region of the galaxy and then adding.

B.3.2
Covariance for coherent broadcasts

For the covariance, I adopt the form (see equation
B9
)

{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]}={[\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}){y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}]^{2}{\cdot}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}{\cdot}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}}\\
{{\cdot}\left[{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(|\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{2}}|)-1\right]{\cdot}\begin{cases}1&\text{if}~p(\upmu_{1})=p(\upmu_{2})\\
\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}&\text{if}~p(\upmu_{1})\neq p(\upmu_{2})\end{cases}.}

(B22)

The coherence function
{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(\Updelta t)=\left\langle|\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{B}}(t)|^{2}|\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{B}}(t+\Updelta t)|^{2}\right\rangle_{\infty}/\left\langle|\mathfrak{a}_{\mathrm{B}}(t)|\right\rangle_{\infty}^{2}
is well-known in quantum optics, describing the covariance between the intensity of electromagnetic radiation at two different times separated by
\Updelta t
in a single polarization
[
46
,
132
,
55
,
131
,
64
, e.g.,]
.
19
19

19

The averaging over the amplitudes and their product is defined to be over a sufficiently long timescale
\gg\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, such that the amplitudes fluctuate chaotically. If the averaging is only done over
\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, the fluctuations will not be accounted for, and
{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}
will appear to be
1
for all sampled
\Updelta t
, just as for a perfectly coherent source.
As an approximation, I adopt a step function:

{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(\Updelta t)-1=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}~\Updelta t\leq\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}/2~\text{and not perfectly coherent}\\
0&\text{if}~\Updelta t>\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}/2~\text{or perfectly coherent}\mathchar 59\relax\end{cases}

(B23)

although the exact form depends on the spectrum of the broadcast
[
131
]
and is identically zero for a perfectly coherent radiation source. The instantaneous bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
of the chord is approximately
\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}^{-1}
. In order for the chord model to be valid, the line needs a coherence time
\gtrsim\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}^{-1}
, though this still leaves open the possibility that
\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}
.

The polarization bears some special discussion. Coherent light is always polarized, and the polarization state remains approximately constant over time intervals shorter than the coherence timescale
[
62
]
. In this sense, coherent broadcasts are always polarized, when viewed on short enough timescales. On timescales longer than
\sim\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, the polarization state may or may not fluctuate, depending on whether the fluctuations in each polarization are correlated. This is what the
\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}
factor describes. If the broadcast is unpolarized on long timescales (like a thermal source with a narrow bandpass filter applied), then the fluctuations in one polarization are independent of those in the other on any delay timescale. This is clear when we consider thermal emission, where the two independent polarizations are essentially different messengers. We then set
\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}=0
. But if the broadcast has a constant polarization on long timescales (e.g., a linear polarizer is placed in front of the transmitter, and we observe both linear polarizations), then the fluctuations are correlated. For example, if the broadcast is fully linear polarized at an angle
45^{\circ}
, the amplitudes in both polarizations will increase and decrease in tandem. For fully polarized broadcasts,
\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}=1
.

Now we must sum over all the modes the broadcast crosses. The modes generally form a grid in time and frequency, with matching points in each polarization. It is clearer to sum along different polarizations first, to bring out all the polarization dependence, before summing along time and frequency. We start by picking one of the polarizations from
\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}
, dubbed
\overline{p}
here, and stripping the observation down to that polarization, for a new window
\mathsf{o}\cap\mathsf{p}(\overline{p})
. The modes in this new window still cover all the time-frequency pairs, and we can use it to define a sum over the time and frequency regions covered by the observation. Equation
B22
then is written as

\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]=[\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}){y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}\cap\mathsf{p}}^{\upmu}(\overline{\mathsf{p}})}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}{\cdot}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\left[{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(|\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{2}}|)-1\right]\\
\cdot\sum_{p_{1}\mathchar 59\relax p_{2}\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}(1\cdot\mathbb{I}[p_{1}=p_{2}]+\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}\cdot\mathbb{I}[p_{1}\neq p_{2}]).

(B24)

Remember,
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}
,
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}
, and
\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}
describe the
mean
emission of the broadcast, not the fluctuations – they should be constants in the chord model, regardless of the instantaneous polarization state. The sum over polarizations can be solved explicitly, noting that
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}(p_{2})|\mathrm{B}}=1-\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}(p_{1})|\mathrm{B}}
for orthogonal polarizations
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
:

\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\sum_{p_{1}\mathchar 59\relax p_{2}\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}(1\cdot\mathbb{I}[p_{1}=p_{2}]+\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}}\cdot\mathbb{I}[p_{1}\neq p_{2}])=\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}-2\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\overline{\mathsf{p}}|\mathrm{B}}(1-\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\overline{\mathsf{p}}|\mathrm{B}})(1-\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathrm{B}})\mathbb{I}[|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|=1].

(B25)

I write the total sum over all modes as

\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]=[\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\upmu}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}){{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}]^{2}{\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B26)

with

\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\frac{1}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}\cap\mathsf{p}}^{\upmu}(\overline{\mathsf{p}})}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}\left[{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(|\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{2}}|)-1\right].

(B27)

As in previous sections, I assume that transmittance solely depends on position. All of the complications in the chord running in and out of the window while competing against the finite coherence time are stuffed into this sum. To do a full calculation for all possible cases is beyond the scope of the paper, because of all the different regimes shown in Figure
7
. Its value can be calculated in some cases:

•

If the broadcast is perfectly coherent, there are no fluctuations to contribute to the covariance, and thus
\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}=0
.

•

If the coherence time is much longer than
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
(which may be longer than the naive
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{CHORD}}
, however), then
g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}{-1}
is always
1
whenever the “chord” is in the observation window, regardless of how dedrifting is affecting things. Then we are simply summing all the
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}
twice, once for
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}
and once for
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}
. Within any chord-like model,
\sum_{\upmu\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
, so
\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}/\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}
.

•

When
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
, then we are working in the true chord model, and the broadcast enters and leaves the window once. The inner summation over
\upmu_{2}
essentially integrates for a duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, subject to additional cutoffs when the chord enters and leaves the observational window:

\sum_{\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}\cap\mathsf{p}}^{\upmu}(\overline{\mathsf{p}})}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}[{g_{p;\mathrm{B}}^{(2)}}(|\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{2}}|)-1]\approx\min(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}+\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}+\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}/2)-\max(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\upmu_{1}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}/2)\approx\min(\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}).

(B28)

Here
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
is when the chord enters the observation window, either because the window itself begins or the chord has drifted into the window’s bandpass;
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}+\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
is when the chord leaves, either because the window ends or it drifts out of the frequency range. There are two natural limits: when
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
, this sum is usually
\sim\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, and when
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}
, it is
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
. The sum over
\upmu_{1}
integrates the time the chord spends in the observational window, which is
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
. Thus,

\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\approx\frac{\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}{\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\min\left(1\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}\right).

(B29)

•

If
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
for a typical “datum” window
\mathsf{d}
used for dedrifting, then the inner sum over
\upmu_{2}
is nonzero usually within only one such datum. Hence we have

\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\approx\frac{\mathnormal{\xi}_{pp;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}}{\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\min\left(1\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathnormal{\tau}_{c;\mathrm{B}}}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{d}|\mathrm{B}}}\right).

(B30)

B.3.3
Variance for coherent broadcast populations

The full expression for the energy variance is found by taking the weighted mean sum of the covariance terms for all the broadcasts in the sample. Applying Campbell’s theorem, the average over broadcast samples for a single society is:

\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]}\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\approx\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle[\mathcal{A}{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{C}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{C}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{E};\mathsf{o}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{C}})]^{2}\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}}\mathbb{I}[\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}].

(B31)

Another application of Campbell’s theorem for the societal distribution, assuming that societies are interchangeable, gives us the average over all samples,

\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\sum_{\upmu_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\upmu_{2}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}{\mathrm{Cov}[\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{1}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathfrak{e}_{\upmu_{2}|\mathrm{B}}]}\right\rangle\approx\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}.

(B32)

Finally, we arrive at the total variance:

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]\approx\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}\left[\left(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}}+\mathcal{A}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right]\\
+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\left(\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}{\left({1}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right).

(B33)

All of the dependence of the variance on the drift rate distribution comes from the term proportional to
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}(1+\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}})
. This sample variance term decreases as the spread of drift rates increases: while more broadcasts are intercepted, the amount of energy caught falls because they cross the window more quickly, and the quadratic dependence on
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}
is the stronger effect.

The terms constant and linear in
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upmu|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
in equation
B33
are known
[
34
, as in]
. One noise variance term in the incoherent expression is missing, reflecting the lack of wave noise in a perfectly coherent signal. Despite the lack of self-interference in individual broadcasts, the mutual interference when there is more than one broadcast adds an additional kind of wave noise. Whenever
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\upmu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\gg\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}/\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}
, equation
61
converges to the incoherent limit of equation
57
. This is, of course, what we expect from the central limit theorem. Furthermore, the partially coherent case (
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\sim}1
) adds a term that effectively magnifies the sample variance, and this term can lead to much greater variance than in the incoherent case.

To understand intuitively how the variance is affected by drift rate, it is helpful to write it in terms of the expected number of broadcasts and individual broadcast signal-to-noise ratio if the drift rate is zero (
\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]
; equation
64
). Most of the terms simplify through the application of conservation of energy (equation
B20
).

\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}(k_{B}\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{n}})^{2}}\approx\left(1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]}{\sqrt{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}}\right)^{2}+\check{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}[\mathfrak{e}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\left[\frac{4{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}}{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}}\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right.\\
\left.\left({1+\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\upmu}}\right)\right]\mathchar 59\relax

(B34)

where
\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}/[\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}]
. Paper I presented calculations for
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
in the true chord model (
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
)

B.3.4
Serendipitous lines in the chord model

Whether or not we have found a line through a deliberate dedrifting search, there is also be a background of “serendipitous” lines. If all lines have the same intrinsic flux, the “brightest” line will be the one that happens to be closest to being dedrifted by accident for whatever
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
is used, the one with the smallest
|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|
if
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
. This can be roughly estimated by calculating the minimum drift rate magnitude expected in a survey. Using the results of Paper I, the minimum drift rate magnitude in a population sampled by window
\mathsf{x}
is found as

F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}})=1-\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\ln\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}+1\right)\right].

(B35)

if
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
is Poissonian (which in general requires the diffuse approximation).

Let us suppose the uniform drift rate distribution holds, with the distribution centered at
\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
and having a width
\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}
(equation
19
). To simplify things further, assume
|\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}-\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}
, and
\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}-|\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}|
, which excludes the possibility that
\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}
spills off the edges of the uniform distribution. It can be shown that

\frac{\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}=\sqrt{\left(\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}^{2}+2\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}+(\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}^{\prime})^{2}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\ln\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}+1\right)\right]\right)+1}-1.

(B36)

The scaled quantities
\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}\equiv\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{M}}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}-\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}})\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
express drift rates in “natural units” of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
.

To calculate the “brightest” expected signal from serendipitous lines, we want to consider all lines caught by all observations of the target galaxy. Thus, the appropriate window to use in equation
B36
covers each point of the sky for the entire dwell time and the entire bandwidth. Now, the bandwidth in a pointing is generally very large in a radio SETI search, at least a few hundred kilohertz if not many megahertz or gigahertz. The frequency window is large enough that broadcasts drift in or out only at the very edge, and
{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax|\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}^{\prime}|}\ll 1
. When
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\gtrsim 1
,

\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}\approx\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\ln 2}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle}\mathchar 59\relax

(B37)

with one line with
|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\approx\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}
expected. As the number of background lines increases, the minimum drift rate magnitude falls proportionally. Eventually, we expect some to be dedrifted by chance. For a fixed
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
with
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
, a line is dedrifted when the long-term smearing effect is smaller than the bandwidth of a channel or the short-term smearing effect,
\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\max(|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}})
. On average, this is achieved when

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\gtrsim\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\ln 2}{\max(|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}})}}{~\text{implying}~\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\gtrsim\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{M}}\ln 2}{\max(|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathchar 59\relax 1)}\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{s}}}\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}.

(B38)

The serendipitous contribution to
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}
then proceeds through the sparse and confusion regimes for these “brightest” dedrifted lines.

Although lines may be sparse enough that the typical trial
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
has no dedrifted lines, the optimal signal-to-noise is found for a specific
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}
that matches the
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
of any specific line. This improvement in performance is modeled with the
{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
variable set to
1
. This line, by careful dedrifting, achieves an effective bandwidth of
\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{min}}\approx\max(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathchar 59\relax|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}})
. But if an observation has intercepted multiple lines, all of equal flux, it is unlikely that any one dedrifted line happens to coincide with the maximum fluctuations in the background of lines. Thus, the optimal signal-to-noise ratio depends on whether the signal-to-noise from an intentionally dedrifted line rises above that of the serendipitous lines.

From these considerations, we have

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}\approx\max\left(\frac{{\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}{\max(1\mathchar 59\relax|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})}\mathchar 59\relax\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}\right)

(B39)

with the serendipitous contribution

\Updelta{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}~\mathbb{M}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]=0\\
\displaystyle\mathrm{max}\left[\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{{\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}}}\right]_{\mathsf{s}|\mathrm{M}}\approx\min\left(\frac{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}(\ln 2)}\mathchar 59\relax 1\right)&\text{if}~\mathbb{M}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]\geq 1~\text{and}~\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}\geq\displaystyle\max\left(|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}\right)\\
\frac{\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}\left[{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}({|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{min}}})\right]_{\mathsf{s}}\right]-\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}({|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{min}}})\right\rangle}{\displaystyle\max(1\mathchar 59\relax|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})}&\text{if}~\mathnormal{\delta}^{L}_{\mathsf{s};\mathrm{M}}<\displaystyle\max\left(|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}}\right)\end{cases}

(B40)

when
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
. When no dedrifting correction is applied (
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
,
\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{min}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}
), and
\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{M}}\geq 1
, it can be shown with the results of Paper I, Appendix C that

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(|\mathnormal{\delta}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\hat{\mathnormal{\beta}}_{\mathsf{o}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}^{\mathrm{min}})\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\frac{3}{2\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{o};\mathrm{M}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B41)

keeping
\Updelta\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathrm{eff}}\sim 1
well past the point at which a low drift rate population would be confused. These effective numbers are defined relative to the brightness of a zero-drift line.

B.3.5
Evaluating sample confusion and sensitivity in surveys for drifting lines

To estimate when sample confusion sets in for a survey, I presuppose
\mathnormal{I}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}=1
and ignore the serendipitous contribution. The sample variance gives an upper limit on the signal-to-noise ratio. Under the usual assumptions (diffuse approximation, identical luminosities, two polarizations observed, distant galaxy,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}
),

{\check{\mathfrak{S}}[\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}}]\lesssim\frac{1}{\max(1\mathchar 59\relax|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})}\left[\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle{\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}})\right]^{-1/2}.}

(B42)

Thus, confusion necessarily sets in for this particular dedrifted observation when

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle>\left[{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}}^{2}\max\left(1\mathchar 59\relax\frac{|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|^{2}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}^{2}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}^{2}}\right)\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\left(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)\right]^{-1}

(B43)

Applying equation
B36
to the observation window, it can be shown that this roughly corresponds to there being
[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\max(1\mathchar 59\relax|\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{d}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}})]^{-1}
serendipitously dedrifted lines per observation.

The conventional assumption is that broadcasts are extremely rare. Thus, we will need to dedrift observations by an amount
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}\approx\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
, typically by
\sim{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}
, a value that may be high for broad drift rate distributions. Yet when there are many broadcasts, some will happen to have a drift rate near
0
by chance, as long as the drift rate distribution extends to zero and the lines have linear drift.

This has two consequences. First, sample confusion only occurs when there are a lot of broadcasts. Because surveys (generally) are made of many observations, there is likely to be a fortuitously dedrifted broadcast for a
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
observation well before confusion sets in. Confusion only prevents detection if it prevents detection
at every tried drift rate, including those near zero
. The “confusion limits” on our sensitivities to broadcasts should therefore be evaluated for
\mathsf{\Delta}_{\mathsf{o}}=0
,

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{0;\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle>\left[\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\mathnormal{\xi}_{\mathnormal{\delta};\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\left(1+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\xi}_{c;\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)\right]^{-1}.}

(B44)

Accordingly, wider drift rate distributions actually preserve our sensitivity against confusion, because more broadcasts are summed into the background of any one observation.

Second, the EIRP sensitivity of a SETI survey is sensitive to smearing. For narrowband observations with incoherent dedrifting, the sensitivity loss can be significant
[
94
]
– for a
typical
line. But for high enough values of
\left\langle{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right\rangle
, a survey is expected to find lines with zero drift, for which the smearing penalty does not apply. Thus, sensitivity loss from drift is dependent on the mean number of broadcasts that are intercepted by the survey. This is basically for the same reason that a survey is more likely to make a detection if there are many broadcasts with a broad luminosity distribution: with so many broadcasts, some will be so far on the high-luminosity tail that they should be detectable.

Lines with significant curvature in their drifts are subject to more smearing, as they do not remain at zero drift for long. This problem would plague even coherent dedrifting and would require more advanced techniques that fit higher-order terms to the drifts. Nonetheless, the same basic points should apply if some lines lack curvature by chance.

Appendix C
Derivation of photon counting variance

This appendix presents a short derivation of the noise variance when counting photons from broadcasts. From equation
A12
, we find the sample variance

\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\right]=\mathcal{A}^{2}\left[\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right]\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\mathchar 59\relax

(C1)

where
{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}
employs the photon distance
{d}_{\mathnormal{q}}={d}_{M}
. For the noise variance, we start by noting that the total number of collected photons is simply a linear sum, with no cross-interference:

\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}=\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}.

(C2)

Furthermore, the broadcasts of a society are independent of each other, and the aggregate emission of the societies in the metasociety are also independent; all are independent of the background noise. Hence, it follows from independence that the individual variances add and

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right].

(C3)

The background noise is almost certainly Poissonian, but it is possible that the variance in the photon broadcasts is not. Sub-Poissonian photon statistics are possible for artificial transmitters, while artificial modulation can greatly increase the variance of individual broadcasts. But if the broadcasts each have Poissonian photon statistics (as unmodulated lasers do),

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right]=\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle.

(C4)

The noise variance is then the mean over all samples. First, the results of Appendix
A
apply; equation
A4
for photons is

\left\langle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\right\rangle=\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\mathcal{A}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\int_{\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{o}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{Q};\mathsf{o}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}){y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}){{\chi}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star})}\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\star}}\right\rangle}{d{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}}d{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}\mathchar 59\relax

(C5)

as long as the societies are interchangeable and extinction depends only on position. Some algebra gives us

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]=\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{A}^{2}\left[\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}\right]\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle\mathchar 59\relax

(C6)

which can also be expressed as

\frac{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{Q}_{\mathsf{o}}\right]}{\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}=1+\frac{\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]}{\sqrt{\left\langle\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathfrak{n}}\right\rangle}}\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{1}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}+\check{\mathfrak{s}}[\mathfrak{q}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}]^{2}\left(\frac{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{o}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\right)\frac{\left\langle{Y}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B};\mathnormal{2}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{q};\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}

(C7)

for convenience when calculating signal-to-noise ratio.

Appendix D
Maximum of many Poisson random variables

Suppose we have a fixed number
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}
of independent samples, each containing
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
events, and we would like to estimate the maximum
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
among those
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}
samples,
{\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]}
. The median
\mathbb{M}\left[{\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]}\right]
provides a typical estimate. Each
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
has a Poisson distribution with a mean
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
. In particular, for equally bright broadcasts and insignificant background, the maximum
\mathfrak{S}
achieved is determined by the difference between the maximum number of broadcasts intercepted by an instrument and the mean.

The cumulative mass function (CMF) of the
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is given by the regularized upper incomplete gamma function,
\mathrm{Q}(n+1\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)
. Then, by extreme value theory (
Gumbel 56
;
Castillo et al. 21
; see Paper I), the maximum of
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}
independent realizations has
\mathnormal{P}(n\leq\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)=\lfloor\mathrm{Q}(n+1\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)\rfloor^{{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}}
. There is no analytic expression for
\left\langle{\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]}\right\rangle
that I am aware of, but the median is defined by

\left\lfloor\mathrm{Q}({\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}+1\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)\right\rfloor=e^{-(\ln 2)/{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}}\approx 1-\frac{\ln 2}{{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(D1)

with the approximation becoming more precise when
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}\gg 1
.

The Poisson distribution function is discrete, leading to jumps in the CMF. Note also that
\mathrm{Q}(n+1\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)
includes the probability that
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}=\lfloor n\rfloor
. I use a continuous probability distribution that treats
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
as a continuous quantity.

D.1
Sparse limit:
\left\langle{N^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\ll 1

When the typical observation is expected to be empty, the Poisson probability falls off exponentially with
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
. The CMF starts out near
1
for
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}=0
, and each time
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is incremented, virtually all of the remainder is eliminated. Thus, to a good approximation,

\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}<n;\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)=\mathrm{Q}(n\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)\approx 1-\frac{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle^{n}}{n!}.

(D2)

By taking advantage of Stirling’s approximation,
n!\approx(n/e)^{n}\sqrt{2\pi n}
, some algebra gives us

{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}\approx\left\lfloor\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\exp\left[1+\mathrm{W}_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle e}\ln\left[\frac{{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}e^{-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle-1}}{\ln 2\sqrt{2\pi\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}}\left(\frac{{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}+1/2}{{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}}\right)^{{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}+1/2}\right]\right)\right]-1/2\right\rfloor

(D3)

using the principal branch of the Lambert
\mathrm{W}
function,
\mathrm{W}_{0}
, and after rounding down because
{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is a discrete variable. The right-hand side depends on
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}
, but the dependence is fairly weak for
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}\gtrsim 1
, approaching
\sqrt{e}
, giving us

{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}\approx\left\lfloor\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\exp\left[1+\mathrm{W}_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle e}\ln\left[\frac{{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}e^{-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle-1/2}}{\ln 2\sqrt{2\pi\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}}\right]\right)\right]-1/2\right\rfloor.

(D4)

Equation
D4
is an excellent approximation when
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}\gg 1
. The argument for
\mathrm{W}_{0}
is generally quite small, but an adequate approximation is
\mathrm{W}_{0}(x)\approx\ln x+\ln\ln x{\cdot}(1/\ln x-1)

[
111
]
. Its argument must be
\geq-1/e
to be valid. Several terms are needed, however; taking
\mathrm{W}_{0}(x)\to\ln x
leads to underestimates of
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}
(Figure
8
).

Figure 8:
Approximations to
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}
in the sparse (left) and confusion (right) regimes. On left, the numerically computed value (solid black; equation
D1
) is compared to estimates (gray): equation
D3
(gray solid; without rounding down, dashed-dotted) and equation
D4
without rounding down (grey, dotted). On right, the numerical result (solid black) compared to equation
D8
(gray dashed) and equation
D4
without rounding (gray dashed-dotted). From top to bottom,
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle=1\mathchar 59\relax 10^{-1}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{-2.5}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{-5}
on the left and
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle=10^{5}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{4}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{3}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{2}\mathchar 59\relax 10^{1}\mathchar 59\relax 1
on the right.

D.2
Confusion limit:
\left\langle{N^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\gg 1

In the confusion limit, the Poisson distribution approaches a normal distribution with mean and variance
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
. Although this approximation does not necessarily hold far out on the tails of the distribution, it suggests that

{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}=\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle+C\sqrt{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}\mathchar 59\relax

(D5)

with
C\sim 1
, because the normal distribution falls off with each standard deviation and outliers will be rare.

Now, for
x=a+\sqrt{2a}C^{\prime}
,

1-\mathrm{Q}(a+1\mathchar 59\relax x)\approx\frac{1}{2}\erfc(-C^{\prime})-\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi a}}(1+{C^{\prime}}^{2})e^{-{C^{\prime}}^{2}}

(D6)

when
a
is large, with
\erfc
referring to the complementary error function
[
105
]
. In this case,
a\to{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}
and
x\to\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
, which gives us
C\approx{C^{\prime}}/\sqrt{2}
. Furthermore, the second term on the right-hand side is small because
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}\approx\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
is large by assumption, and it is suppressed rapidly as
u
increases past
1
. Thus,

u\approx\sqrt{2}\erf^{-1}\left[2\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{1/{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}}-1\right].

(D7)

For large
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}
, this can be written as
C^{2}\approx\mathrm{W}_{0}[{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}^{2}/(2\pi(\ln 2)^{2})]
. The first term of
\mathrm{W}_{0}(x)
gives us the relatively simple

C\sim\sqrt{2\ln\frac{{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\ln 2}}.

(D8)

This is a poor approximation when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\sim 1
, but it does well for large
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
(Figure
8
, dashed lines).

Remarkably, equation
D4
also does well in the confusion regime when
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}\gg 1
(dash-dotted lines), despite equation
D2
being a poor approximation. More accurately,
\mathrm{Q}(n+1\mathchar 59\relax\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle)\approx 1-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle^{n+1}/(n+1)!{\cdot}C\sqrt{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}
where
C\leq\sqrt{\pi/2}
decreases with
{\mathsf{N}^{\mathsf{o}}}
, but the
{\mathbb{M}\left[\mathrm{max}[\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{J}}]\right]}
only has a logarithmic dependence on the additional factor.

D.3
Applicability to populations of broadcasts

These approximations are used in this paper to estimate the fluctuations in the number of broadcasts for the signal-to-noise ratio. Broadcasts are not generally Poisson; they are clustered into societies. We can ignore this when the diffuse approximation is applicable (
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\ll 1
). Then,
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}
is basically a Bernoulli variable flagging the rare societies that are broadcasting in the
\mathsf{o}
window; the societies of a metasociety are a Poisson point process, and a Poisson sum of Bernoulli variables is itself Poisson. The approximations also apply if the number of societies is fixed: perhaps we posit a single society for a survey covering a single star that we are trying to constrain, much like the single metasociety assumption applying for a target galaxy in this paper.

The approximations also apply in a way for the opposite limit, when
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\gg\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle
and
\gg 1
, if all societies are interchangeable (with
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
). Then, there are small deviations in the number of broadcasts per society, but very nearly all societies have the same number. The fluctuations in the signal-to-noise ratio are mostly the result of the varying number of societies, with
\mathbb{M}\left[\max_{\mathsf{o}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}}{{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\right]\approx\mathbb{M}\left[\max_{\mathsf{o}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}}{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right]\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
. Note, however, that the number of effective observations is much smaller, roughly the number of nonoverlapping pointings – measurements in different channels and likely even different epochs sample the same societies.
